<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

The continuous Hochschild cohomology of an operator algebra measures the obstruction to solving its bounded cocycle equations. The theory also connects these equations with stability of multiplication under small perturbations (Sinclair and Smith 1995, vol. 203, chap. 7) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[SinclairSmith1995](#ref-SinclairSmith1995)]. For a von Neumann algebra acting on itself by multiplication, the degree-one equation is the derivation equation, and its vanishing is the inner-derivation theorem of Kadison and Sakai (Kadison 1966; Sakai 1966) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Kadison1966](#ref-Kadison1966), [Sakai1966](#ref-Sakai1966)]. The higher-degree theory was developed by Johnson, Kadison, and Ringrose (Kadison and Ringrose 1971b, 1971a; Johnson, Kadison, and Ringrose 1972) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[KadisonRingrose1971](#ref-KadisonRingrose1971), [KadisonRingrose1971II](#ref-KadisonRingrose1971II), [JKR1972](#ref-JKR1972)]. Throughout, boundedness means ordinary operator-norm boundedness of a multilinear map. No complete-boundedness condition is imposed.

For a complex von Neumann algebra $M$, let $C_b^k(M,M)$ be the Banach space of bounded complex $k$-linear maps $M^k\to M$, with norm $\norm{f}=\sup_{\norm{a_1},\ldots,\norm{a_k}\leq1}
\norm{f(a_1,\ldots,a_k)}$, and put $C_b^0(M,M)=M$. For $k\geq1$, the differential is <a id="eq:differential"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
(df)(a_1,\ldots,a_{k+1})
={}&a_1f(a_2,\ldots,a_{k+1})\notag\\
&+\sum_{j=1}^k(-1)^j
 f(a_1,\ldots,a_ja_{j+1},\ldots,a_{k+1})\notag\\
&+(-1)^{k+1}f(a_1,\ldots,a_k)a_{k+1}.
\label{eq:differential}
\end{aligned}$$ In degree zero, $(db)(a)=ab-ba$. As usual, $d^2=0$. We use the notation $$H_b^k(M,M)=
\ker\bigl(d:C_b^k(M,M)\to C_b^{k+1}(M,M)\bigr)
\big/
\operatorname{im}\bigl(d:C_b^{k-1}(M,M)\to C_b^k(M,M)\bigr).$$ The denominator is the actual image, without a closure.

The Kadison–Ringrose cohomology conjecture asks whether $H_b^k(M,M)=0$ for every complex von Neumann algebra $M$ and every positive integer $k$. Sinclair and Smith trace this self-coefficient formulation to 1967 (Sinclair and Smith 1998b, 3376) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[SinclairSmith1998](#ref-SinclairSmith1998)]. Our main result settles the higher degrees.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *Let $M$ be a complex von Neumann algebra and let $k\geq2$. Every $f\in C_b^k(M,M)$ satisfying $df=0$ has the form $f=dg$ for some $g\in C_b^{k-1}(M,M)$. Consequently $H_b^k(M,M)=0$.*

</div>

Together with the inner-derivation theorem, Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> resolves the Kadison–Ringrose cohomology conjecture positively.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Prior work

Kadison and Ringrose proved vanishing for type I and hyperfinite von Neumann algebras (Kadison and Ringrose 1971b, Theorem 4.4) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[KadisonRingrose1971](#ref-KadisonRingrose1971)] <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[KadisonRingrose1971II](#ref-KadisonRingrose1971II)] (Kadison and Ringrose 1971a, Theorem 3.1). Their extended-cobounding theorem also gives a primitive for an $M$-valued cocycle on a general concrete von Neumann algebra $M\subset B(H)$ when the primitive may take values in $B(H)$ (Kadison and Ringrose 1971a, Theorem 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[KadisonRingrose1971II](#ref-KadisonRingrose1971II)]; keeping the values in the original algebra is the essential self-coefficient problem here. Johnson, Kadison, and Ringrose then identified continuous cohomology with separately normal cohomology (Johnson, Kadison, and Ringrose 1972, Theorem 5.6) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[JKR1972](#ref-JKR1972)]. The completely bounded methods of Christensen, Effros, and Sinclair (Christensen, Effros, and Sinclair 1987) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[CES1987](#ref-CES1987)] established vanishing whenever the type $\mathrm{II}_1$ central summand absorbs the hyperfinite $\mathrm{II}_1$ factor, including all algebras without a type $\mathrm{II}_1$ summand; see the precise restatement in (Christensen et al. 2003, 636, equation (1.1)) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[CPSS2003](#ref-CPSS2003)].

The remaining finite-algebra problem has several substantial positive cases. Sinclair and Smith proved vanishing for algebras with separable predual and a Cartan subalgebra (Sinclair and Smith 1998a) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[SinclairSmith1998Cartan](#ref-SinclairSmith1998Cartan)]; Cameron removed the separability hypothesis for type $\mathrm{II}_1$ factors with a Cartan maximal abelian subalgebra (Cameron 2009, Theorem 3.2) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Cameron2009](#ref-Cameron2009)]. Christensen, Pop, Sinclair, and Smith proved vanishing for all type $\mathrm{II}_1$ factors with property $\Gamma$, including nonseparable ones (Christensen et al. 2003, Theorem 7.2) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[CPSS2003](#ref-CPSS2003)]. Qian and Shen extended this direction to type $\mathrm{II}_1$ algebras with separable predual and their generalized property $\Gamma$ (Qian and Shen 2015, Theorem 6.4) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[QianShen2015](#ref-QianShen2015)]. In degree two, Pop and Smith proved vanishing for the tensor product of any two type $\mathrm{II}_1$ von Neumann algebras, with no separability assumption (Pop and Smith 2010, Theorem 4.5) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[PopSmith2010](#ref-PopSmith2010)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> removes the structural restrictions on the finite algebra in every degree $k\geq2$.

The Cartan argument of Sinclair and Smith also provides a useful methodological comparison. They show that complete boundedness in the last cocycle variable suffices. They use a projection of Christensen and Sinclair (Christensen and Sinclair 1995) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[ChristensenSinclair1995](#ref-ChristensenSinclair1995)] from completely bounded linear maps onto right module maps, in the form recalled in (Sinclair and Smith 1998b, Theorem 3.4) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[SinclairSmith1998](#ref-SinclairSmith1998)], to obtain a primitive (Sinclair and Smith 1998b, sec. 4) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[SinclairSmith1998](#ref-SinclairSmith1998)]. Thus the passage from averaging one variable to a module identity already has a central role in the earlier theory.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The new ingredients

<a id="eq:intro-harmonic"></a>
We obtain the needed module identity by averaging over a single unitary random walk. Averaging one cocycle input first gives only a harmonic map; the main new input is a Liouville theorem that makes such a map commute with right multiplication. Suppose that $M$ is a type $\mathrm{II}_1$ algebra with separable predual and faithful normal tracial state $\tau$, and write $\norm{x}_2=\tau(x^*x)^{1/2}$. Let $\mathcal U(M)$ denote its unitary group. We construct a symmetric, countably supported probability measure $\mu$ on $\mathcal U(M)$, with a $2$-dense set of atoms, such that $$\label{eq:intro-harmonic}
T(x)=\mathbb E_{U\sim\mu}\bigl[T(xU)U^*\bigr]
\quad(x\in M)
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
T(x)=T(1)x.$$ Here $T:M\to M$ is any bounded linear map that is $2$-norm continuous on each operator-norm ball. No positivity or complete-boundedness assumption is imposed. The measure is fixed before the harmonic map is chosen.

For a separately normal cocycle, Akemann’s continuity theorem (Akemann 1967, Corollary III.10) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Akemann1967](#ref-Akemann1967)] supplies the stated continuity in the last input. A uniform continuity modulus survives the averaging limit, even though that limit need not remain normal, and also passes to the tracial ultrapowers used in the Liouville proof.

The proof of <a href="#eq:intro-harmonic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-harmonic">[eq:intro-harmonic]</a> combines probabilistic and algebraic arguments. The finite free configurations come from Popa’s construction of free-independent sequences (Popa 1995) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Popa1995](#ref-Popa1995)], in the formulation of his later ultraproduct independence theorem (Popa 2014) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Popa2014](#ref-Popa2014)]. These give finite unitary configurations with prescribed small word traces. A hierarchy of increasingly rare, increasingly large sets of such unitaries gives a single random walk whose long independent products approach free Haar joint moments along a subsequence. Only moment convergence is required.

Second, we use the classical harmonic-martingale construction (Furstenberg 1963, 415) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Furstenberg1963](#ref-Furstenberg1963)] to obtain an $L^2(M,\tau)$-valued martingale. A nonconstant limit would produce free Haar unitaries $g_1,g_2,\ldots$ in a tracial ultrapower and distinct $a,b\in M$. The coordinatewise lift of $T$ to that ultrapower sends each nonempty signed word $w$ with pairwise distinct unsigned indices to $aw$ or $bw$, according to the sign of its first letter; here $a,b$ are identified with their constant images. We prove that these identities are impossible for a bounded map with the stated continuity. The argument averages free generators, discards repeated surviving indices using Haagerup’s free-group length estimate (Haagerup 1979) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Haagerup1979](#ref-Haagerup1979)], and passes to a unitary polar part. Positive and negative powers of that unitary would then carry different left multipliers. Uniformly bounded sine sums and a spectral partition force the multipliers to agree. This rigidity statement, proved in Theorem <a href="#thm:rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:rigidity">11</a>, is independent of the cocycle problem.

Finally, in this tracial type $\mathrm{II}_1$ setting, for a separately normal cocycle $f$ we take a pointwise ultraweak cluster point of the Cesàro averages in its last input. The Liouville theorem identifies the resulting fixed map as a right module map in that input. Averaging the original cocycle equation then gives a primitive $g$ with $\norm{g}\leq\norm{f}$. Together with compactness, this uniform estimate removes the separability assumption. We then partition the type $\mathrm{II}_1$ summand into central pieces admitting faithful normal tracial states. Lemma <a href="#lem:central-homotopy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:central-homotopy">20</a> corrects mixed inputs from distinct pieces, and uniform bounds permit their assembly. The classical vanishing theorem handles the complementary summand.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Conventions and classical reductions

All von Neumann algebras and maps are complex, and subalgebras are unital unless otherwise stated. A finite algebra is called type $\mathrm{II}_1$ here when it has no nonzero type I central summand; its center need not be trivial. A map $f:M^k\to M$ is *separately normal* if it is ultraweakly continuous in each input with the others fixed. For a finite algebra with faithful normal tracial state, the $2$-norm on bounded sets determines the strong-star topology. The inherited traces on subalgebras and tracial ultrapowers will usually also be denoted by $\tau$.

We use the following established cohomology results.

<div class="samepage">

<a id="stmt-prop-classical"></a>
<div id="prop:classical" class="proposition">

**Proposition 2** (Classical reductions). *Let $M$ be a von Neumann algebra and $k\geq2$.*

1.  *If $f\in C_b^k(M,M)$ is a cocycle, there are a separately normal bounded cocycle $f_0$ and a bounded $(k-1)$-cochain $b$ such that $f=f_0+db$.*

2.  *If $M$ has no type $\mathrm{II}_1$ central summand, then $H_b^k(M,M)=0$.*

</div>

</div>

Part <span class="upright">(i)</span> is the Johnson–Kadison–Ringrose normal-reduction theorem (Johnson, Kadison, and Ringrose 1972, Lemma 5.4 and Theorem 5.6) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[JKR1972](#ref-JKR1972)]; its formulation for self-coefficients is recalled in (Christensen et al. 2003, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[CPSS2003](#ref-CPSS2003)]. Part <span class="upright">(ii)</span> combines the classical type I and properly infinite vanishing results; see (Kadison and Ringrose 1971b, Theorem 4.4) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[KadisonRingrose1971](#ref-KadisonRingrose1971)] <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[CES1987](#ref-CES1987)], (Christensen, Effros, and Sinclair 1987), and (Christensen et al. 2003, 636, equation (1.1)). Both statements concern ordinary continuous cohomology and the actual image of $d$. These are the only cohomological inputs in the proof.

Section <a href="#sec:walk" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:walk">2</a> constructs the walk. Section <a href="#sec:liouville" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:liouville">3</a> proves its Liouville property using Theorem <a href="#thm:rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:rigidity">11</a>, proved in Section <a href="#sec:rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:rigidity">4</a>. Section <a href="#sec:cohomology" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cohomology">5</a> constructs bounded primitives and completes the reductions to arbitrary $M$.

<a id="sec:walk"></a>
### A random walk with asymptotically free products

Throughout this section, $(M,\tau)$ is a finite von Neumann algebra of type $\mathrm{II}_1$ with separable predual and faithful normal tracial state. In particular, $M$ may have a nontrivial center. We construct a single symmetric probability measure on $\mathcal U(M)$ whose independent long products approach the joint moments of free Haar unitaries along a subsequence. The construction combines finite approximation to freeness with increasingly rare increments chosen at successive stages.

Recall that unital subalgebras of a tracial von Neumann algebra are *free* if every alternating product of centered elements from successively different subalgebras has trace zero. Here centered means having trace zero. A unitary $u$ is *Haar* if $\tau(u^k)=0$ for every $k\in\mathbb Z\setminus\{0\}$. A free Haar family $(u_i)$ is equivalently characterized by $$\tau\bigl(w(u_1,\ldots,u_r)\bigr)=0
 \qquad(w\in\mathbb F_r\setminus\{e\}),$$ for every finite subfamily, where $\mathbb F_r$ denotes the free group on $r$ specified generators. The equivalence follows by expanding centered Laurent polynomials and then taking bounded $2$-norm approximations. The length $|w|$ is its reduced word length.

For a probability measure $\mu$ on $\mathcal U(M)$, let $\mu^{*n}$ be the law of the ordered product $U_1\cdots U_n$ of independent increments of law $\mu$. Symmetry means that $U^{-1}$ has the same law as $U$.

<a id="stmt-thm-walk"></a>
<div id="thm:walk" class="theorem">

**Theorem 3**. *Let $M$ be a finite type $\mathrm{II}_1$ von Neumann algebra with separable predual and faithful normal tracial state $\tau$. There exist a symmetric, countably supported probability measure $\mu$ on $\mathcal U(M)$ and a strictly increasing sequence $n_\ell\to\infty$ with the following properties.*

*The positive-mass atoms of $\mu$ contain a countable $2$-norm dense subset of $\mathcal U(M)$, and hence generate $M$ as a von Neumann algebra. For every $r\in\mathbb N$, every nonempty finite set $\mathcal W\subset\mathbb F_r\setminus\{e\}$, and every $\eta>0$, if $G_1^{(\ell)},\ldots,G_r^{(\ell)}$ are independent with law $\mu^{*n_\ell}$, then $$\Pr\left(
   \max_{w\in\mathcal W}
   \left|\tau\bigl(w(G_1^{(\ell)},\ldots,G_r^{(\ell)})\bigr)\right|
   \geq\eta
 \right)\longrightarrow0.$$ Thus these products have joint free Haar moments in probability.*

</div>

We first obtain finite unitary configurations for which prescribed words in path products have small traces. We then place such configurations at increasingly rare levels of a single measure, arranging that each finite moment test succeeds with probability tending to one.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Finite requirements from ultrapower freeness

The freeness input originates in Popa’s free-independent sequence construction (Popa 1995, Lemma 1.4 and Theorem 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[Popa1995](#ref-Popa1995)]. We use the following special case of its later ultraproduct formulation (Popa 2014, Theorem 0.1(a)) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[Popa2014](#ref-Popa2014)].

<a id="eq:tracial-ultrapower"></a>
For a tracial von Neumann algebra $(P,\tau_P)$ and a free ultrafilter $\omega$ on $\mathbb N$, its tracial ultrapower is $$\label{eq:tracial-ultrapower}
 P^\omega=\ell^\infty(\mathbb N,P)/\mathcal I_\omega,
 \qquad
 \mathcal I_\omega
 =\{(x_m):\lim_{m\to\omega}\norm{x_m}_2=0\}.$$ It is a finite von Neumann algebra with faithful normal trace $\tau_P^\omega((x_m)^\omega)=\lim_{m\to\omega}\tau_P(x_m)$. We identify $P$ with its constant sequences.

<a id="stmt-lem-popa-special"></a>
<div id="lem:popa-special" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Popa’s ultrapower freeness). *Let $P$ be a $\mathrm{II}_1$ factor and let $B\subset P^\omega$ be a unital von Neumann subalgebra with separable predual. There is a diffuse abelian von Neumann subalgebra of $P^\omega$ free from $B$. Consequently, for every $d\in\mathbb N$, there are Haar unitaries $u_1,\ldots,u_d\in P^\omega$ such that $B,W^*(u_1),\ldots,W^*(u_d)$ are free.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-popa-special"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-popa-special</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Theorem 0.1(a) of (Popa 2014) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[Popa2014](#ref-Popa2014)] with every coordinate algebra and every coordinate subalgebra equal to $P$. The required non-intertwining condition holds: a nonzero corner of the diffuse factor $P$ cannot embed in the scalar relative commutant, or in a finite matrix amplification of it. Moreover, $P^\omega$ is a factor, so the relative commutant of the full ultraproduct subalgebra is $\mathbb C1$. The conclusion for the separable centered subspace $B\ominus\mathbb C1$ is therefore ordinary scalar-trace freeness. This is the case in which every coordinate subalgebra equals its ambient factor, discussed after that theorem and in (Popa 2014, secs. 1.5–1.6) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[Popa2014](#ref-Popa2014)].

A diffuse abelian tracial algebra contains a Haar unitary. Choose one, adjoin it to $B$, and repeat. Finite adjunction preserves separable predual: a countable generating set gives a separable tracial Hilbert space by polynomial approximation. Thus all $d$ choices take place in the same ultrapower. At each step the new algebra is free from the algebra generated at the preceding steps, giving the asserted family. ◻

</details>

We next record why a single fresh label in each path is sufficient. A *path expression* is a finite ordered product of fixed unitary coefficients and formal letters $U_j^{\pm1}$. Occurrences and labels in such expressions are counted before any simplification.

<a id="stmt-lem-distinguished-letters"></a>
<div id="lem:distinguished-letters" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Distinguished letters). *Let $B,W^*(u_1),\ldots,W^*(u_d)$ be free subalgebras of a tracial von Neumann algebra, with each $u_j$ Haar. Let $y_1,\ldots,y_r$ be path expressions evaluated at these unitaries, with coefficients in $\mathcal U(B)$. Suppose that for each $i$ there is a label $h_i$ occurring exactly once in the entire list of path expressions, and that its occurrence belongs to $y_i$. Then $$\tau\bigl(w(y_1,\ldots,y_r)\bigr)=0
 \qquad(w\in\mathbb F_r\setminus\{e\}).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-distinguished-letters"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-distinguished-letters</summary>

*Proof.* The labels $h_1,\ldots,h_r$ are distinct. Put all nondistinguished unitaries into the coefficient algebra: $$B_0=W^*\bigl(B,u_j:j\notin\{h_1,\ldots,h_r\}\bigr),
 \qquad
 C=W^*(u_{h_1},\ldots,u_{h_r}).$$ Then $B_0$ and $C$ are free. This grouping property follows directly by expanding centered polynomials in a free family into alternating centered products; bounded approximation extends it to the generated von Neumann algebras. Write $$y_i=A_i u_{h_i}^{\epsilon_i}B_i,
 \qquad A_i,B_i\in\mathcal U(B_0),\quad \epsilon_i\in\{-1,1\}.$$ If $w=x_{i_1}^{\sigma_1}\cdots x_{i_t}^{\sigma_t}$ is nonempty and reduced, its evaluated product has the form $$c_0 u_{h_{i_1}}^{\epsilon_{i_1}\sigma_1}c_1
 \cdots u_{h_{i_t}}^{\epsilon_{i_t}\sigma_t}c_t,
 \qquad c_0,\ldots,c_t\in\mathcal U(B_0).$$ The displayed distinguished-letter string is itself reduced. Indeed, an adjacent inverse pair would require $i_j=i_{j+1}$ and $\sigma_j=-\sigma_{j+1}$, contrary to the reduction of $w$.

Expand every $c_j$, including the two outside coefficients, as its scalar part plus its centered part. In each resulting term, removing scalar coefficients joins distinguished letters into nonempty contiguous subwords of that reduced string. Every such block is a centered element of $C$. The remaining factors alternate between centered elements of $B_0$ and centered elements of $C$, and there is at least one $C$ factor. Freeness makes the trace zero. If there are no centered coefficient factors, the term is just a scalar multiple of a nonempty reduced Haar word and again has trace zero. This proves the assertion, with no condition on the first and last indices of $w$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-finite-path-tests"></a>
<div id="lem:finite-path-tests" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Simultaneous finite path requirements). *Fix a finite set $F\subset\mathcal U(M)$, an integer $d\geq1$, and $\varepsilon>0$. Consider a finite collection of tests. Each test consists of a finite list of path expressions $y_1,\ldots,y_r$ in the alphabet $F\cup\{U_j^{\pm1}:1\leq j\leq d\}$ and a nonidentity word $w\in\mathbb F_r$. Assume that each path in each test has a distinguished label occurring exactly once in that test’s entire list, as in Lemma <a href="#lem:distinguished-letters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:distinguished-letters">5</a>. The distinguished labels may differ between tests. There are $u_1,\ldots,u_d\in\mathcal U(M)$ for which all the tests satisfy $$\left|\tau\bigl(w(y_1(u),\ldots,y_r(u))\bigr)\right|
 <\varepsilon.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-finite-path-tests"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-finite-path-tests</summary>

*Proof.* First suppose that $M=P$ is a factor. The constant coefficient algebra $W^*(F)\subset P^\omega$ has separable predual. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:popa-special" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:popa-special">4</a> and then Lemma <a href="#lem:distinguished-letters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:distinguished-letters">5</a>. The same free $d$-tuple makes every prescribed trace exactly zero in $P^\omega$, even though different tests can designate different labels.

Every unitary of $P^\omega$ has unitary representatives. To see this, take a bounded self-adjoint logarithm there, lift it to a bounded sequence of self-adjoint elements of $P$, and exponentiate coordinatewise. Choose such representatives for the $d$ unitaries. Each test trace converges to zero along $\omega$. Since the family of tests is finite, the coordinate sets on which their absolute values are less than $\varepsilon$ have a common member. Its $d$ coordinate unitaries solve all the requirements in $P$.

For general $M$, the factor case will give successful tuples in almost every fiber. The remaining task is to choose them measurably. Use the central direct integral (Blackadar 2006, vol. 122, sec. III.1.6 and III.5.1) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[Blackadar2006](#ref-Blackadar2006)] $$M=\int_\Omega^\oplus M_s\,d\nu(s),
 \qquad
 \tau(x)=\int_\Omega\tau_s(x(s))\,d\nu(s),$$ where $(\Omega,\nu)$ is a probability space and, almost everywhere, $M_s$ is a $\mathrm{II}_1$ factor with its normalized trace $\tau_s$. Standard separable direct-integral theory provides countably many bounded measurable sections $a_j$ whose values generate $M_s$ for almost every $s$. After removing one null set, all these properties and the unitary identities for the finitely many coefficients hold simultaneously.

There is a countable family $(v_j)$ of measurable unitary sections which is dense in each fiber unitary group for $\norm{\cdot}_{2,s}$. Here is an explicit construction. Enumerate the sections $$\exp(iq(a_1,a_2,\ldots)),$$ where $q$ ranges over self-adjoint $*$-polynomials in finitely many variables with coefficients in $\mathbb Q+i\mathbb Q$. This includes symmetrizations of arbitrary rational $*$-polynomials. In a fixed fiber, any unitary is $\exp(ih)$ for a bounded self-adjoint $h$. Kaplansky density and norm approximation by rational polynomials approximate $h$ in $2$-norm by the indicated self-adjoint polynomials. For bounded self-adjoint $a,b$, the integral identity $$e^{ia}-e^{ib}
 =i\int_0^1 e^{i(1-t)a}(a-b)e^{itb}\,dt$$ implies $\norm{e^{ia}-e^{ib}}_2\leq\norm{a-b}_2$. Thus these sections have the required fiberwise density. Each section is globally bounded, and exponentiation makes its norm one.

Enumerate all ordered $d$-tuples drawn from $(v_j)$ as $\boldsymbol v^{(1)},\boldsymbol v^{(2)},\ldots$. Let $E_k$ be the set of fibers where candidate $k$ satisfies every test with absolute trace less than $\varepsilon/2$. These sets are measurable, since the traces of products of measurable sections are measurable. They cover almost every fiber. Indeed, the factor case gives a successful tuple in each such fiber, and the requirements are open in the product $2$-norm topology. For this last assertion, telescoping products of unitaries gives $$\norm{z_1\cdots z_L-z'_1\cdots z'_L}_2
 \leq\sum_{j=1}^L\norm{z_j-z'_j}_2,
 \qquad |\tau_s(x)|\leq\norm{x}_{2,s},$$ and inversion is also a $2$-norm isometry on unitaries. The countable dense tuples therefore meet the open set of successful choices.

Take the first successful tuple: on the disjoint measurable sets $$E_k\setminus\bigcup_{j<k}E_j$$ use $\boldsymbol v^{(k)}$, and use the identity tuple on the null complement. The resulting sections $u_1,\ldots,u_d$ are measurable and unitary, so belong to $M$. Each test has fiber trace of absolute value less than $\varepsilon/2$ almost everywhere. Integrating gives an absolute global trace at most $\varepsilon/2<\varepsilon$, as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### One measure satisfying all moment tests

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-finite-path-tests-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-finite-path-tests</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:walk" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:walk">3</a>.* Choose a $2$-norm dense sequence $(v_j)_{j\geq1}$ in $\mathcal U(M)$ and put $$\mu_0=\sum_{j\geq1}2^{-j-1}(\delta_{v_j}+\delta_{v_j^{-1}}).$$ For $\ell\geq1$, fix the numerical parameters $$\begin{aligned}
 p_\ell&=2^{-2^\ell},
 &p_0&=1-\sum_{\ell\geq1}p_\ell,\\
 n_\ell&=p_\ell^{-3/2}=2^{3\cdot2^{\ell-1}},
 &K_\ell&=\ell n_\ell,\\
 d_\ell&=K_\ell^4.
\end{aligned}$$ Here $n_\ell$ and $d_\ell$ are positive integers, and $p_0>0$ because $p_\ell\leq4^{-\ell}$. At time $n_\ell$ we will sample $\ell$ paths, choosing level $j$ with probability $p_j$ at each increment. These parameters make level $\ell$ appear in every path with high probability while later levels remain unlikely. The much larger label count $d_\ell$ makes repeated level-$\ell$ labels unlikely across the $K_\ell$ increments. We construct finite symmetric measures $$\mu_\ell=\frac1{2d_\ell}
   \sum_{j=1}^{d_\ell}(\delta_{u_{\ell,j}}
                            +\delta_{u_{\ell,j}^{-1}})$$ successively, and finally set $$\mu=p_0\mu_0+\sum_{\ell\geq1}p_\ell\mu_\ell.$$

At stage $\ell$, choose a finite symmetric set $F_\ell$ of base atoms with $\mu_0(F_\ell)>1-K_\ell^{-2}$. The coefficient alphabet consists of $F_\ell$ and all $u_{j,h}^{\pm1}$ from stages $j<\ell$; it is finite. Introduce $d_\ell$ unknown unitary labels. Consider every ordered list of $\ell$ paths of length $n_\ell$ in this alphabet and the signed unknown labels such that:

1.  each path contains at least one unknown label;

2.  no unknown label occurs twice anywhere in the entire list, irrespective of sign.

For each such list and every nonempty reduced word of length at most $\ell$ on $\ell$ generators, require its evaluated trace to have absolute value less than $1/\ell$. There are finitely many lists and finitely many words: all alphabet sizes, path lengths, and word lengths have been fixed before making this choice. Designating any one unknown occurrence in each path puts every test under Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-path-tests" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-path-tests">6</a>. Apply that lemma to choose $u_{\ell,1},\ldots,u_{\ell,d_\ell}$ satisfying all these requirements simultaneously. This completes the induction.

We estimate probabilities on a labeled sampling space. An increment first chooses a level $j$ with probability $p_j$. At level zero it chooses an atom with law $\mu_0$; at level $j\geq1$ it chooses one of $d_j$ labels uniformly and an independent uniform sign. Its value is then the corresponding unitary. This description gives law $\mu$ even when distinct labels happen to represent the same element of $M$.

<a id="eq:walk-bad-events"></a>
Sample $\ell$ independent paths of length $n_\ell$, involving $K_\ell$ increments in all. Let $\mathcal B_\ell$ be the union of the following four events: a level greater than $\ell$ appears; a level-zero increment lies outside $F_\ell$; a level-$\ell$ label is repeated; or some path has no level-$\ell$ increment. Union bounds give $$\begin{aligned}
 \Pr(\mathcal B_\ell)
 &\leq K_\ell\sum_{j>\ell}p_j
       +K_\ell p_0\mu_0(\mathcal U(M)\setminus F_\ell)\notag\\
 &\quad+\frac{\binom{K_\ell}{2}}{d_\ell}
       +\ell(1-p_\ell)^{n_\ell}.
 \label{eq:walk-bad-events}
\end{aligned}$$ For the collision term, any specified pair of increments has probability $p_\ell^2/d_\ell\leq1/d_\ell$ of using the same level-$\ell$ label; the signs do not matter. For the last term, a specified path misses that level with probability $(1-p_\ell)^{n_\ell}$.

Every term on the right of <a href="#eq:walk-bad-events" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:walk-bad-events">[eq:walk-bad-events]</a> tends to zero. Indeed, $p_{\ell+1}=p_\ell^2$ and $p_\ell\leq1/4$, so $$\sum_{j>\ell}p_j
 \leq\frac{p_\ell^2}{1-p_\ell^2}
 \leq\frac{16}{15}p_\ell^2.$$ Consequently, the four terms admit the respective bounds $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{16}{15}\ell\sqrt{p_\ell},\qquad
 K_\ell^{-1},\qquad
 \frac{1}{2K_\ell^2},\qquad
 \ell\exp(-p_\ell^{-1/2}),
\end{aligned}$$ all converging to zero.

Outside $\mathcal B_\ell$, the sampled paths form exactly one of the lists tested at stage $\ell$. All their earlier-level factors belong to the coefficient alphabet, each path has a level-$\ell$ label, and all occurrences of those labels are distinct. Thus, simultaneously for every $w\in\mathbb F_\ell$ with $1\leq|w|\leq\ell$, the trace of the evaluated word has absolute value less than $1/\ell$.

Now fix $r$, a nonempty finite $\mathcal W$, and $\eta>0$. For $\ell\geq\max\{r,\max_{w\in\mathcal W}|w|\}$, append independent paths to the given $r$ paths to obtain $\ell$ paths. On the preceding good event every word in $\mathcal W$ has absolute trace below $1/\ell$. Once $1/\ell<\eta$, the probability in the theorem is at most $\Pr(\mathcal B_\ell)$ and therefore tends to zero.

The measure $\mu$ is symmetric and countably supported by construction. Its base component has positive weight, so each $v_j$ is a positive-mass atom. Since bounded $2$-norm convergence implies strong convergence in the tracial representation, the von Neumann algebra generated by these atoms contains every unitary and hence is $M$. Finally, the identity group word always has trace one, and every $*$-monomial in unitaries reduces to a group word. The asserted convergence therefore describes all joint $*$-moments. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rem-walk-only-moments"></a>
<div id="rem:walk-only-moments" class="remark">

*Remark 7*. Theorem <a href="#thm:walk" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:walk">3</a> asserts convergence of scalar moments. It does not assert convergence of operator norms of polynomials in the sampled products. Its finite-family formulation permits further conditions whose failure probabilities tend to zero to be imposed simultaneously at any fixed stage of a later selection argument.

</div>

<a id="sec:liouville"></a>
### A Liouville theorem for the walk

We show that a bounded harmonic map for the walk of Theorem <a href="#thm:walk" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:walk">3</a>, subject to continuity on bounded sets in the tracial $2$-norm, is left multiplication by its value at $1$. The probabilistic part of the proof produces free Haar unitaries such that, on words with pairwise distinct unsigned indices, a single map acts by one of two left multipliers according to the sign of the first letter. Theorem <a href="#thm:rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:rigidity">11</a>, proved in Section <a href="#sec:rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:rigidity">4</a>, forces those multipliers to coincide for bounded linear maps with the same continuity in any finite von Neumann algebra with a faithful normal tracial state.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Continuity and tracial ultrapowers

<a id="stmt-def-ball-continuity"></a>
<div id="def:ball-continuity" class="definition">

<a id="eq:ball-sequential"></a>
**Definition 8**. Let $(N,\rho)$ be a finite von Neumann algebra with a faithful normal tracial state. A linear map $R:N\to N$ is *bounded-ball $2$-continuous* if $$\label{eq:ball-sequential}
 \sup_j\norm{x_j}<\infty,\qquad \norm{x_j}_2\longrightarrow0
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \norm{R(x_j)}_2\longrightarrow0.$$ Here and below $\norm{x}_2=\rho(x^*x)^{1/2}$ when the ambient algebra is $(N,\rho)$.

</div>

The following lemma records the continuity needed after each of the two ultrapowers in the argument. It applies without a separability assumption.

<a id="stmt-lem-ultrapower"></a>
<div id="lem:ultrapower" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:ball-modulus"></a>
<a id="eq:ultrapower-map"></a>
**Lemma 9**. *Let $(N,\rho)$ be as in Definition <a href="#def:ball-continuity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:ball-continuity">8</a>, and let $R:N\to N$ be bounded and linear. Condition <a href="#eq:ball-sequential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ball-sequential">[eq:ball-sequential]</a> is equivalent to the following uniform assertion: for every $r<\infty$ and $\varepsilon>0$ there is $\delta>0$ such that $$\label{eq:ball-modulus}
 \norm{x}\le r,\quad \norm{x}_2<\delta
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \norm{R(x)}_2<\varepsilon.$$ If this condition holds and $\omega$ is a free ultrafilter on $\mathbb N$, then $$\label{eq:ultrapower-map}
 R^\omega:N^\omega\longrightarrow N^\omega,
 \qquad R^\omega([(x_j)]_\omega)=[(R(x_j))]_\omega,$$ is a well-defined bounded linear map with $\norm{R^\omega}\le\norm{R}$. It is bounded-ball $2$-continuous for the ultrapower trace. In particular, for every free ultrafilter $\nu$, the map $(R^\omega)^\nu$ on $(N^\omega)^\nu$ has the same properties.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-ultrapower"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-ultrapower</summary>

*Proof.* If <a href="#eq:ball-modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ball-modulus">[eq:ball-modulus]</a> fails for some $r,\varepsilon$, choose $x_j$ with $\norm{x_j}\le r$, $\norm{x_j}_2<1/j$, and $\norm{R(x_j)}_2\ge\varepsilon$. This contradicts <a href="#eq:ball-sequential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ball-sequential">[eq:ball-sequential]</a>; the converse is immediate. Applying the uniform assertion to differences gives uniform $2$-norm continuity on each fixed operator norm ball.

Use the tracial ultrapower and its ideal $\mathcal I_\omega$ from <a href="#eq:tracial-ultrapower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tracial-ultrapower">[eq:tracial-ultrapower]</a>, now for $(N,\rho)$. Its tracial norm is $$\norm{[(x_j)]_\omega}_2=\lim_{j\to\omega}\norm{x_j}_2.$$ By <a href="#eq:ball-modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ball-modulus">[eq:ball-modulus]</a>, the coordinate map induced by $R$ preserves $\mathcal I_\omega$. Applying this observation to the difference of two bounded representatives proves that <a href="#eq:ultrapower-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ultrapower-map">[eq:ultrapower-map]</a> is well-defined. The quotient norm gives $$\norm{R^\omega(X)}
 \le \norm{R}\inf_{[(x_j)]_\omega=X}\sup_j\norm{x_j}
 =\norm{R}\norm{X}.$$

To transfer the modulus, fix $r<\infty$ and $\varepsilon>0$, and choose $\delta$ in <a href="#eq:ball-modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ball-modulus">[eq:ball-modulus]</a> for the radius $r+1$ and the output tolerance $\varepsilon/2$. If $\norm{X}\le r$, the quotient norm allows a representative with $\sup_j\norm{x_j}\le r+1$. When $\norm{X}_2<\delta/2$, the set $$\{j:\norm{x_j}_2<\delta\}$$ belongs to $\omega$. Consequently $$\norm{R^\omega(X)}_2
 =\lim_{j\to\omega}\norm{R(x_j)}_2
 \le\varepsilon/2<\varepsilon.$$ Thus $\delta/2$ is a valid input tolerance for $R^\omega$ on the radius-$r$ ball. The proof used no separability of $N$ and applies again with $N^\omega$ in its place, proving the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### The harmonic map and its martingale

The probabilistic step uses the classical passage from harmonic functions to martingales along random products; see (Furstenberg 1963, 415) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[Furstenberg1963](#ref-Furstenberg1963)]. Here the function is $D(g)=T(g)g^*$ with values in $L^2(M,\tau)$, and the required convergence follows directly from orthogonality of Hilbert-space martingale increments.

<a id="stmt-thm-liouville"></a>
<div id="thm:liouville" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:harmonic-map"></a>
<a id="eq:liouville-conclusion"></a>
**Theorem 10**. *Let $(M,\tau)$ be a type $\mathrm{II}_1$ von Neumann algebra with separable predual and a faithful normal tracial state, and let $\mu$ be the measure supplied by Theorem <a href="#thm:walk" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:walk">3</a>. Suppose that $T:M\to M$ is bounded, linear, and bounded-ball $2$-continuous, and that $$\label{eq:harmonic-map}
 T(x)=\mathbb E_{U\sim\mu}\bigl[T(xU)U^*\bigr]
 \qquad(x\in M).$$ Then $$\label{eq:liouville-conclusion}
 T(x)=T(1)x\qquad(x\in M).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-liouville"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-liouville</summary>

<a id="eq:martingale-cauchy"></a>
*Proof.* Write $C=\norm{T}$ and $H=L^2(M,\tau)$. Expectations in <a href="#eq:harmonic-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:harmonic-map">[eq:harmonic-map]</a> are norm-convergent sums, since $\mu$ is countably supported and $\norm{T(xU)U^*}\le C\norm{x}$. For $g\in\mathcal U(M)$ put $$D(g)=T(g)g^*.$$ Let $U_1,U_2,\ldots$ be independent with law $\mu$, and set $W_0=1$, $W_n=U_1\cdots U_n$, and $\mathcal F_n=\sigma(U_1,\ldots,U_n)$. The $H$-valued random variables $Z_n=D(W_n)$ have countable range and satisfy $\norm{Z_n}_2\le\norm{Z_n}\le C$. Moreover, $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E[Z_{n+1}\mid\mathcal F_n]
 &=\mathbb E\bigl[T(W_nU_{n+1})U_{n+1}^*W_n^*
                  \mid\mathcal F_n\bigr] \\
 &=T(W_n)W_n^*=Z_n.
\end{aligned}$$ Thus $(Z_n)$ is a martingale in $L^2(\Omega;H)$. Its increments are orthogonal, and $$\norm{Z_0}_2^2+
 \sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\mathbb E\norm{Z_{j+1}-Z_j}_2^2
 =\mathbb E\norm{Z_n}_2^2\le C^2.$$ It follows that $Z_n$ converges in $L^2(\Omega;H)$ to a random variable $Z_\infty$, and continuity of conditional expectation gives $Z_n=\mathbb E[Z_\infty\mid\mathcal F_n]$. In particular, with <a id="eq:martingale-error"></a>$$\label{eq:martingale-error}
 e_k=\mathbb E\norm{Z_\infty-Z_k}_2^2\longrightarrow0,$$ orthogonality gives the uniform Cauchy estimate $$\label{eq:martingale-cauchy}
 \mathbb E\norm{Z_n-Z_k}_2^2=e_k-e_n\le e_k
 \qquad(n\ge k).$$

The operator norm ball $K_C=\{x\in M:\norm{x}\le C\}$ is closed in $H$. Indeed, from a $2$-norm-convergent sequence in $K_C$, ultraweak compactness provides an ultraweakly convergent subnet with limit in $K_C$. Testing against $\tau(y^*\,\cdot)$, for $y\in M$, identifies this limit with the $H$-limit, since $M$ is dense in $H$. A subsequence of $Z_n$ converges almost surely in $H$, so $Z_\infty\in K_C$ almost surely.

If $Z_\infty=c$ almost surely, conditional expectation gives $Z_0=Z_1=c$ almost surely. Hence $c=T(1)$ and $D(u)=c$ at every atom $u$ of $\mu$. These atoms contain a $2$-norm-dense subset of $\mathcal U(M)$ by Theorem <a href="#thm:walk" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:walk">3</a>. For any $u\in\mathcal U(M)$, choose atoms $u_j$ with $\norm{u_j-u}_2\to0$. Bounded-ball continuity gives $T(u_j)\to T(u)$ in $2$-norm, while $\norm{cu_j-cu}_2\le\norm{c}\norm{u_j-u}_2\to0$. Since $T(u_j)=cu_j$, we obtain $T(u)=cu$. Every element of a unital complex $C^*$-algebra is a finite linear combination of unitaries, proving <a href="#eq:liouville-conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:liouville-conclusion">[eq:liouville-conclusion]</a> in this case.

Suppose, for a contradiction, that $Z_\infty$ is not deterministic. Its law is a Borel probability measure on the separable Hilbert space $H$, so its support contains two distinct points $a,b$. Because $K_C$ is closed and has full measure, $a,b\in K_C\subset M$.

<a id="eq:two-endpoints"></a>
*Forward and reverse endpoints.* We claim that, for every $\delta>0$, there are numbers $\eta_\delta>0$ and $N_\delta$ such that, whenever $n\ge N_\delta$ and $G$ has law $\mu^{*n}$, $$\label{eq:two-endpoints}
 \Pr\bigl(\norm{D(G)-a}_2<\delta,
          \ \norm{D(G^{-1})-b}_2<\delta\bigr)
 \ge\eta_\delta.$$ Realize $G=U_1\cdots U_n$, let $h=\lfloor n/2\rfloor$, and put $$A_n=D(U_1\cdots U_h),\qquad
 B_n=D(U_n^{-1}\cdots U_{n-h+1}^{-1}).$$ The two variables use disjoint sets of increments and are independent. Symmetry of $\mu$ implies that both have the law of $Z_h$. Apply <a href="#eq:martingale-cauchy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:martingale-cauchy">[eq:martingale-cauchy]</a> to the forward string and separately to the reversed inverse string, which is also a string of independent increments with law $\mu$. It gives <a id="eq:endpoint-replacement"></a>$$\label{eq:endpoint-replacement}
 \mathbb E\norm{D(G)-A_n}_2^2\le e_h,
 \qquad
 \mathbb E\norm{D(G^{-1})-B_n}_2^2\le e_h.$$ For fixed $\delta$, the support property gives $$p_a=\Pr(\norm{Z_\infty-a}_2<\delta/3)>0,
 \qquad
 p_b=\Pr(\norm{Z_\infty-b}_2<\delta/3)>0.$$ Mean-square convergence implies, for all sufficiently large $h$, $$\Pr(\norm{Z_h-a}_2<2\delta/3)\ge p_a/2,
 \qquad
 \Pr(\norm{Z_h-b}_2<2\delta/3)\ge p_b/2.$$ Independence of $A_n,B_n$, the triangle inequality, and Markov’s inequality therefore bound the probability in <a href="#eq:two-endpoints" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:two-endpoints">[eq:two-endpoints]</a> from below by $$\frac{p_ap_b}{4}-\frac{18e_h}{\delta^2}.$$ For large $n$ this is at least $p_ap_b/8$, proving the claim. The independence used here is that of the disjoint prefixes $A_n,B_n$; the variables $G$ and $G^{-1}$ remain dependent.

<a id="eq:independent-blocks"></a>
*Concatenation of independent blocks.* Let $G_1,\ldots,G_m$ be independent with law $\mu^{*n}$, and consider $$v(G)=G_{i_1}^{\sigma_1}\cdots G_{i_t}^{\sigma_t},
 \qquad \sigma_j\in\{1,-1\},$$ where $i_1,\ldots,i_t$ are pairwise distinct. Each inverse block can be represented by reversing and inverting its own increments. By symmetry, concatenating these distinct signed blocks is a length-$tn$ walk with independent increments of law $\mu$. In particular, $$\bigl(D(v(G)),D(G_{i_1}^{\sigma_1})\bigr)
 \quad\hbox{has the law of}\quad (Z_{tn},Z_n).$$ Consequently $$\label{eq:independent-blocks}
 \mathbb E\norm{D(v(G))-D(G_{i_1}^{\sigma_1})}_2^2\le e_n.$$ The restriction is on unsigned indices: using both $G_i$ and $G_i^{-1}$ would reuse a block and would not justify this estimate.

*Finite requirements and diagonal selection.* Fix an integer $m\ge1$ first, and use tolerance $1/m$ in <a href="#eq:two-endpoints" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:two-endpoints">[eq:two-endpoints]</a>. For $m$ independent length-$n$ products, let $E_{m,n}$ be the event $$\norm{D(G_i)-a}_2<1/m,\qquad
 \norm{D(G_i^{-1})-b}_2<1/m
 \quad(1\le i\le m).$$ Independence between the paths gives numbers $c_m>0$ and $N_m$ such that <a id="eq:conditioning-mass"></a>$$\label{eq:conditioning-mass}
 \Pr(E_{m,n})\ge c_m\qquad(n\ge N_m).$$ For example, one may take $c_m=(\eta_{1/m})^m$.

<a id="eq:selected-moments"></a>
<a id="eq:selected-blocks"></a>
Let $\mathcal Q_m$ be the finite set of nonidentity reduced words of length at most $m$ on the symbols $x_1,\ldots,x_m$. Let $\mathcal V_m$ be the finite set of all nonempty signed words on these symbols with pairwise distinct unsigned indices. Along the times $n_\ell$ in Theorem <a href="#thm:walk" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:walk">3</a>, the probability that any of the requirements $$\begin{aligned}
 |\tau(q(G_1,\ldots,G_m))|&<1/m
       &&(q\in\mathcal Q_m),\label{eq:selected-moments}\\
 \norm{D(v(G))-D(G_{i_1}^{\sigma_1})}_2&<1/m
       &&\bigl(v=x_{i_1}^{\sigma_1}\cdots x_{i_t}^{\sigma_t}
                   \in\mathcal V_m\bigr)\label{eq:selected-blocks}
\end{aligned}$$ fails tends to zero. For the first line, this is a finite union of the moment convergence statements in Theorem <a href="#thm:walk" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:walk">3</a>. For the second line, the union bound and <a href="#eq:independent-blocks" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:independent-blocks">[eq:independent-blocks]</a> give the upper bound $|\mathcal V_m|m^2e_{n_\ell}$.

Choose $\ell_m>\ell_{m-1}$, after fixing $m$, so large that $n_{\ell_m}\ge N_m$ and this combined failure probability is less than $c_m/2$. Then the intersection of $E_{m,n_{\ell_m}}$ with all the requirements <a href="#eq:selected-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selected-moments">[eq:selected-moments]</a>–<a href="#eq:selected-blocks" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selected-blocks">[eq:selected-blocks]</a> has probability at least $c_m/2>0$. Choose a deterministic tuple $$(u_1^{(m)},\ldots,u_m^{(m)})\in\mathcal U(M)^m$$ from that intersection. The constants $c_m$ may tend to zero as fast as they please: the choice of $\ell_m$ is made separately for each fixed $m$, after the positive lower bound has been obtained.

<a id="eq:selected-multipliers"></a>
Write $a_1=a$ and $a_{-1}=b$. The endpoint conditions and <a href="#eq:selected-blocks" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selected-blocks">[eq:selected-blocks]</a> imply, for every $v=x_{i_1}^{\sigma_1}\cdots x_{i_t}^{\sigma_t}\in\mathcal V_m$, $$\label{eq:selected-multipliers}
 \norm{T(v(u^{(m)}))-a_{\sigma_1}v(u^{(m)})}_2
 =\norm{D(v(u^{(m)}))-a_{\sigma_1}}_2<2/m.$$ The equality uses $T(v)=D(v)v$ and the $2$-norm isometry of right multiplication by the unitary $v$.

*The exact family in the ultrapower.* Fix a free ultrafilter $\omega_1$ on the tuple index $m$. Set $u_i^{(m)}=1$ when $m<i$, and define $$N=M^{\omega_1},\qquad
 g_i=[(u_i^{(m)})_{m\ge1}]_{\omega_1}\quad(i\ge1),
 \qquad R=T^{\omega_1}.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:ultrapower" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ultrapower">9</a> makes $R$ a bounded linear map on $N$, with $\norm{R}\le C$, and supplies its bounded-ball $2$-continuity. Each $g_i$ is unitary. Every fixed nonidentity reduced word $q$ lies in $\mathcal Q_m$ for all sufficiently large $m$, so <a href="#eq:selected-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selected-moments">[eq:selected-moments]</a> gives <a id="eq:exact-haar-family"></a>$$\label{eq:exact-haar-family}
 \tau^{\omega_1}(q(g_1,g_2,\ldots))=0.$$ These are precisely the joint moments of a countable free Haar family: powers of one generator are centered, and expansion of alternating centered Laurent polynomials in different generators reduces to nonidentity words. The corresponding freeness of their generated von Neumann algebras follows by bounded $2$-norm approximation.

<a id="eq:first-letter-multipliers"></a>
Identify $a,b\in M$ with their constant images in $N$. For every nonempty word $w=g_{i_1}^{\sigma_1}\cdots g_{i_t}^{\sigma_t}$ with pairwise distinct unsigned indices, <a href="#eq:selected-multipliers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selected-multipliers">[eq:selected-multipliers]</a> holds for all sufficiently large tuple indices. Hence it becomes the exact identity $$\label{eq:first-letter-multipliers}
 R(w)=
 \begin{cases}
  aw,&\sigma_1=1,\\
  bw,&\sigma_1=-1.
 \end{cases}$$ These are all the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:rigidity">11</a>, applied to $(N,\tau^{\omega_1})$ and $R$. That theorem yields $a=b$ in $N$. The constant embedding preserves the faithful trace, so this also means $a=b$ in $M$, contrary to their choice. Thus $Z_\infty$ is deterministic, and the earlier argument proves <a href="#eq:liouville-conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:liouville-conclusion">[eq:liouville-conclusion]</a>. ◻

</details>

Only the scalar joint moments of the $g_i$ and the identities <a href="#eq:first-letter-multipliers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:first-letter-multipliers">[eq:first-letter-multipliers]</a> enter the rigidity theorem. Freeness of these unitaries from $a$ or $b$ is not asserted or needed.

<a id="sec:rigidity"></a>
### Rigidity of the first-letter identities

The identities furnished by the walk concern words whose unsigned indices are pairwise distinct. We prove that their two left multipliers must agree. This rules out the distinct martingale endpoints constructed in Section <a href="#sec:liouville" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:liouville">3</a>.

<a id="stmt-thm-rigidity"></a>
<div id="thm:rigidity" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:rigidity-first-letter"></a>
**Theorem 11**. *Let $(N,\tau)$ be a finite von Neumann algebra with a faithful normal tracial state. Let $R:N\to N$ be bounded and complex linear, and suppose that <a id="eq:rigidity-continuity"></a>$$\label{eq:rigidity-continuity}
 \norm{R(x_n)}_2\longrightarrow0
 \quad\text{whenever}\quad
 \sup_n\norm{x_n}<\infty,
 \qquad \norm{x_n}_2\longrightarrow0.$$ Let $(g_j)_{j\geq1}$ be a free Haar family in $N$, and let $a,b\in N$. Suppose that for every nonempty signed word $w=g_{i_1}^{\varepsilon_1}\cdots g_{i_q}^{\varepsilon_q}$ with $\varepsilon_j\in\{1,-1\}$ and pairwise distinct indices $i_1,\ldots,i_q$, $$\label{eq:rigidity-first-letter}
 R(w)=
 \begin{cases}
 aw,&\varepsilon_1=1,\\
 bw,&\varepsilon_1=-1.
 \end{cases}$$ Then $a=b$.*

</div>

Here the free Haar condition means that the trace of every nonidentity reduced group word in the $g_j$ is zero. In particular, distinct reduced words are orthonormal for the tracial $2$-norm. The coefficients $a,b$ are arbitrary elements of the containing algebra.

The target is a unitary whose positive powers carry the multiplier $a$ and whose negative powers carry the multiplier $b$. A spectral argument will show that these identities force $a=b$ for a bounded map in a finite tracial algebra. Higher powers of any one $g_j$ repeat its unsigned index, so the hypothesis does not give this target directly. Instead, we average many free generators, control the repeated indices in suitable polynomial expressions, and pass to the polar part of the resulting element in a further tracial ultrapower.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Norm control for free words

We use the following form of Haagerup’s length estimate (Haagerup 1979, Lemma 1.4) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[Haagerup1979](#ref-Haagerup1979)]. We give the proof, including the transfer from the regular representation to the containing tracial algebra.

<a id="stmt-lem-rigidity-length"></a>
<div id="lem:rigidity-length" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:rigidity-length"></a>
**Lemma 12**. *Let $\mathbb F_r$ be the free group on $r$ generators and let $\lambda$ be its left regular representation. For a finitely supported family $(c_w)_{|w|=q}$ on the reduced words of length $q\geq0$, $$\label{eq:rigidity-length}
 \left\|\sum_{|w|=q}c_w\lambda(w)\right\|
 \leq(q+1)\left(\sum_{|w|=q}|c_w|^2\right)^{1/2}.$$ If $g_1,\ldots,g_r$ have free Haar joint moments in a faithfully tracial von Neumann algebra, every scalar-coefficient word polynomial in them has the same norm as the corresponding polynomial in the regular generators. Consequently, <a href="#eq:rigidity-length" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-length">[eq:rigidity-length]</a> also holds with $\lambda(w)$ replaced by $w(g)$, and its right-hand side is $(q+1)\norm{\sum_{|w|=q}c_w w(g)}_2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rigidity-length"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rigidity-length</summary>

*Proof.* Write $L=\sum_{|w|=q}c_w\lambda(w)$. On the standard basis of $\ell^2(\mathbb F_r)$, separate each product $wx$ according to its exact number $t\in\{0,\ldots,q\}$ of cancellations at the joining boundary. This gives $L=\sum_{t=0}^q L_t$ on finitely supported vectors. Fix $t$. Such a product has the form $$w=uv,\qquad x=v^{-1}h,\qquad wx=uh,
 \qquad |u|=q-t,\quad |v|=t,$$ where all three displayed concatenations $uv$, $v^{-1}h$, and $uh$ are reduced. Conversely, these conditions describe exactly the products with $t$ cancellations, allowing any of $u,v,h$ to be empty when its required length permits this. In particular, $h$ may be empty when the input is exhausted.

For this fixed $t$, an input basis word of length at least $t$ has a unique prefix of length $t$ and hence a unique suffix $h$. An output basis word of length at least $q-t$ likewise determines $h$ uniquely by removing its prefix of length $q-t$. Thus the input spaces indexed by different $h$ are orthogonal, as are the corresponding output spaces. Relative to these two decompositions, $L_t$ is a direct sum of matrices $B_h$, with columns indexed by $v$ and rows by $u$. An entry is $c_{uv}$ when the three reduction conditions hold and is zero otherwise. The fixed cutting length makes $(u,v)\mapsto uv$ injective within each matrix. Therefore $$\norm{B_h}\leq\norm{B_h}_{\mathrm{HS}}
 \leq\left(\sum_{|w|=q}|c_w|^2\right)^{1/2}.$$ Taking the direct-sum norm bounds $L_t$ by the same quantity. Summing over the $q+1$ possible values of $t$ proves <a href="#eq:rigidity-length" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-length">[eq:rigidity-length]</a>.

<a id="eq:rigidity-norm-moments"></a>
For the norm transfer, recall that if $A\geq0$ belongs to a von Neumann algebra with faithful tracial state $\rho$, then $$\label{eq:rigidity-norm-moments}
 \norm{A}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\rho(A^n)^{1/n}.$$ The upper bound is immediate. If $0<c<\norm{A}$, the spectral projection $e=1_{(c,\infty)}(A)$ is nonzero and has positive trace, so $\rho(A^n)\geq c^n\rho(e)$. Taking roots and then letting $c\uparrow\norm{A}$ proves the formula. For a word polynomial $p$, the free Haar moments give, for every $n\geq1$, $$\tau\bigl((p(g)^*p(g))^n\bigr)
 =\tau_{\mathbb F_r}\bigl((p(\lambda)^*p(\lambda))^n\bigr).$$ Both traces are faithful; here $\tau_{\mathbb F_r}$ is the canonical trace on the free group von Neumann algebra. Applying <a href="#eq:rigidity-norm-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-norm-moments">[eq:rigidity-norm-moments]</a> gives $\norm{p(g)}=\norm{p(\lambda)}$. The asserted $2$-norm formula follows from orthonormality of reduced words. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:rigidity-second-ultrapower"></a>
Under the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:rigidity">11</a>, set <a id="eq:rigidity-normalized-sums"></a>$$\label{eq:rigidity-normalized-sums}
 s_r=r^{-1/2}\sum_{j=1}^r g_j\in N.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:rigidity-length" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rigidity-length">12</a> gives $\norm{s_r}_2=1$ and $\norm{s_r}\leq2$ for every $r$. Fix a free ultrafilter $\omega_2$ on $\mathbb N$ and put $$\label{eq:rigidity-second-ultrapower}
 \widehat N=N^{\omega_2},\qquad
 \widehat\tau=\tau^{\omega_2},\qquad
 s=(s_r)_{\omega_2}\in\widehat N,\qquad S=R^{\omega_2}.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:ultrapower" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ultrapower">9</a> ensures that $S$ is bounded and retains $2$-norm continuity on operator norm balls. We identify $a,b$ with their constant images in $\widehat N$.

In the application from Section <a href="#sec:liouville" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:liouville">3</a>, $N=M^{\omega_1}$ and $\widehat N=(M^{\omega_1})^{\omega_2}$. The first index $m$ selects increasingly accurate unitary tuples; the second index $r$ increases the number of exact free generators being averaged in $N$, where the bound $\norm{s_r}\leq2$ is already available. The hypotheses and notation of Theorem <a href="#thm:rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:rigidity">11</a> remain in force through the next two subsections.

Figure <a href="#fig:proof-map" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:proof-map">1</a> locates the two ultrapowers in the Liouville argument and records the remaining passage from these bounded sums to a polar unitary and then to $a=b$.
