<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-proof-map"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:proof-map`)

Visual witness description of PDF page 15 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Flowchart diagram enclosed in a vertical sequence of 5 rectangular boxes connected by downward-pointing arrows with text labels between the boxes:
- Box 1: "The fixed walk has asymptotically free moments. A hypothetical nonconstant harmonic-map martingale has distinct support points $a, b \in M$."
- Downward arrow labeled: "$\downarrow$ finite selection and the first ultrapower"
- Box 2: "In $N = M^{\omega_1}$: exact free Haar unitaries $g_1, g_2, \dots$ and exact first-letter identities for $R = T^{\omega_1}$."
- Downward arrow labeled: "$\downarrow$ free-group length estimate in $N$"
- Box 3: "$s_r = r^{-1/2} \sum_{j=1}^r g_j$ satisfies $\|s_r\| \le 2$. In each fixed product of blocks $s_r(s_r^* s_r)^d$, $d \ge 0$, terms with repeated surviving indices have total norm tending to zero."
- Downward arrow labeled: "$\downarrow$ the second ultrapower and polar approximation"
- Box 4: "In $\widehat{N} = N^{\omega_2}$: $s = (s_r)_{\omega_2}$ has a unitary polar part $V$, and $S = R^{\omega_2}$ satisfies $S(V^k) = a V^k$, $S(V^{-k}) = b V^{-k}$."
- Downward arrow labeled: "$\downarrow$ bounded sine sums and the finite faithful trace"
- Box 5: "$a = b$, a contradiction. Hence $T(x) = T(1)x$. Last-input averaging then produces the tracial cocycle primitive."
```


<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### From distinct indices to ordered blocks

Polynomial expressions in $s_r$ may contain repeated indices, so their multiplier identities do not follow directly from the hypothesis. For polar approximation we will need powers of $sp(s^*s)$, where $p$ is a scalar polynomial. Expanding these powers without commuting any factors gives products of the blocks $B_d(z)=z(z^*z)^d$, $d\geq0$. We establish their identities by showing that repeated surviving indices contribute an error tending to zero in operator norm.

<a id="stmt-lem-rigidity-blocks"></a>
<div id="lem:rigidity-blocks" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:rigidity-block-identities"></a>
**Lemma 13**. *For every $k\geq1$ and $d_1,\ldots,d_k\geq0$, let $$P(z)=B_{d_1}(z)B_{d_2}(z)\cdots B_{d_k}(z).$$ Then $$\label{eq:rigidity-block-identities}
 S(P(s))=aP(s),\qquad S(P(s)^*)=bP(s)^*.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rigidity-blocks"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rigidity-blocks</summary>

*Proof.* Fix this product, and let $L=\sum_{j=1}^k(2d_j+1)$ be its degree. Every term in the expansion of one block $B_d(s_r)$ has the odd alternating sign pattern $$+,-,+,-,\ldots,+.$$ Deleting adjacent inverse letters deletes two consecutive opposite signs. The remaining pattern is still alternating, begins and ends positively, and is nonempty: the excess of positive over negative signs remains one. Hence the free reduction of each block is a nonempty word with positive endpoint signs. After reducing the blocks internally, their boundaries join two positive letters and admit no further cancellation. Every reduced word in $P(s_r)$ is therefore nonempty and begins and ends positively. Taking adjoints reverses the block order and all signs, so the corresponding assertion for $P(s_r)^*$ has negative endpoint signs.

We next bound the coefficient of every possible reduced word, including words much shorter than $L$. Write $$P(s_r)=\sum_{q=1}^L\sum_{|w|=q}c_w(r)w(g).$$ For a fixed reduced word $w$ of length $q$, record a free reduction of an original term by pairing its original positions whenever their letters cancel. For definiteness, the reduction may be performed by reading from left to right and canceling against the last surviving letter whenever possible. The recorded pairs form a noncrossing partial matching of the $L$ positions: there are $(L-q)/2$ pairs, and the unmatched positions spell $w$ in order. All nested and newly adjacent cancellations are included in this record.

<a id="eq:rigidity-coefficient-bound"></a>
There are at most $3^L$ noncrossing partial matchings of $L$ positions. Indeed, mark each position as an opening endpoint, a closing endpoint, or unmatched; noncrossing forces a closing endpoint to pair with the last unpaired opening endpoint, so this encoding is injective. We may count every such partial matching, including ones not realized by a reduction. For a fixed pattern and fixed $w$, the labels at the unmatched positions are prescribed. Each canceled pair has equal unsigned labels and opposite signs and hence has at most $r$ label choices. Coincidences between different pairs or with unmatched positions can only reduce the number of choices. Each original term has coefficient $r^{-L/2}$. Consequently, for every $q$ and $w$, $$\label{eq:rigidity-coefficient-bound}
 |c_w(r)|\leq C_L r^{(L-q)/2}r^{-L/2}
 =C_Lr^{-q/2},\qquad C_L=3^L.$$ An impossible parity or reduction pattern contributes zero. The same bound applies to the adjoint polynomial.

<a id="eq:rigidity-discard-norm"></a>
Let $E_{r,q}$ be the sum of those length-$q$ terms whose *surviving reduced word* has a repeated unsigned index. Original repetitions which disappear in the reduction remain included in the coefficients of the retained words. For $q=1$ this sum is zero. For $q\geq2$ the number of possible words in the sum is at most $$2^q\binom q2 r^{q-1}:$$ choose a pair of equal-index positions, choose their common label and the other $q-2$ labels, and choose all signs. Counting nonreduced strings here only enlarges the bound. By orthonormality and <a href="#eq:rigidity-coefficient-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-coefficient-bound">[eq:rigidity-coefficient-bound]</a>, <a id="eq:rigidity-discard-ltwo"></a>$$\label{eq:rigidity-discard-ltwo}
 \norm{E_{r,q}}_2^2
 \leq C_L^2 2^q\binom q2 r^{-1}\qquad(2\leq q\leq L).$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:rigidity-length" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rigidity-length">12</a> now yields the aggregate operator norm estimate $$\label{eq:rigidity-discard-norm}
 E_r=\sum_{q=1}^L E_{r,q},\qquad
 \norm{E_r}\leq\sum_{q=2}^L(q+1)\norm{E_{r,q}}_2
 =O_L(r^{-1/2})\longrightarrow0.$$ There is no scalar term, by the block reduction argument.

Put $Q_r=P(s_r)-E_r$. Each word in this finite sum has pairwise distinct unsigned indices and begins positively. Thus <a href="#eq:rigidity-first-letter" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-first-letter">[eq:rigidity-first-letter]</a> and linearity give $R(Q_r)=aQ_r$. Moreover, $\norm{P(s_r)}\leq2^L$, so <a href="#eq:rigidity-discard-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-discard-norm">[eq:rigidity-discard-norm]</a> also proves that the sequence $(Q_r)$ is bounded. In $N$ we have $$\norm{R(P(s_r))-aP(s_r)}
 \leq(\norm{R}+\norm{a})\norm{E_r}\longrightarrow0.$$ Passing to $\widehat N$ proves the first identity in <a href="#eq:rigidity-block-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-block-identities">[eq:rigidity-block-identities]</a>. For the adjoint, the retained sum is $Q_r^*$: its reduced words have distinct unsigned indices and begin negatively. Therefore $R(Q_r^*)=bQ_r^*$, and the identical estimate with $b$ proves the second identity. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### A polar unitary and all its powers

The spectral calculation below is related to the polar decomposition of circular elements in free probability, established by Voiculescu and also proved by Banica (Voiculescu 1990; Banica 1996) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[Voiculescu1990Circular](#ref-Voiculescu1990Circular), [Banica1996Polar](#ref-Banica1996Polar)]. We derive the required Catalan moments and spectral distribution directly. Only the absence of an atom at zero is needed to obtain the unitary polar part.

<a id="stmt-lem-rigidity-polar"></a>
<div id="lem:rigidity-polar" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:rigidity-power-identities"></a>
**Lemma 14**. *The polar part $V$ of $s$ is a unitary in $\widehat N$, and $$\label{eq:rigidity-power-identities}
 S(V^k)=aV^k,\qquad S(V^{-k})=bV^{-k}
 \qquad(k\geq1).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rigidity-polar"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rigidity-polar</summary>

*Proof.* We first show that $s$ has zero kernel, which makes its polar part unitary in the finite algebra. We then transfer the block identities to powers of that unitary. Set $A=s^*s$, so $0\leq A\leq4$. To determine its kernel, we compute its moments. For $d\geq1$, the quantity $\tau((s_r^*s_r)^d)$ is $r^{-d}$ times the number of assignments of labels in $\{1,\ldots,r\}$ to the alternating $2d$ positions $$-,+,-,+,\ldots,-,+$$ whose word reduces to the identity. An identity reduction pairs all positions noncrossingly, so it uses at most $d$ unsigned labels. Assignments using at most $d-1$ labels number $O_d(r^{d-1})$: for each number of labels, choose an ordered list of those labels and then assign a member of that list to each of the $2d$ positions.

If exactly $d$ labels occur in an identity word, every label occurs twice. Its two occurrences must be paired in any cancellation matching, so the matching is uniquely determined by the labeling. Conversely, in a noncrossing perfect matching, every pair has an even number of positions inside it, since those positions must be matched among themselves. The endpoints therefore have opposite parities, and hence opposite signs in our alternating string. Assigning distinct labels to its $d$ pairs gives an identity word: erase innermost pairs successively. It follows that the number of leading assignments is exactly $$C_d(r)_d,\qquad (r)_d=r(r-1)\cdots(r-d+1),$$ where $C_d$ is the number of noncrossing perfect matchings on $2d$ positions. Choosing the partner of the first position gives $C_0=1$ and $C_d=\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}C_jC_{d-1-j}$. The generating function thus satisfies $C(z)=1+zC(z)^2$; its solution with constant term one gives $$C_d=\frac1{d+1}\binom{2d}{d}.$$ Consequently, <a id="eq:rigidity-catalan-moments"></a>$$\label{eq:rigidity-catalan-moments}
 \widehat\tau(A^d)
 =\lim_{r\to\omega_2}\tau((s_r^*s_r)^d)
 =\lim_{r\to\infty}r^{-d}
       \bigl(C_d(r)_d+O_d(r^{d-1})\bigr)
 =C_d.$$ The zeroth moment is also $C_0=1$.

<a id="eq:rigidity-mp-law"></a>
Consider the measure $$\label{eq:rigidity-mp-law}
 d\nu(x)=\frac1{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{4-x}{x}}\,
                1_{(0,4)}(x)\,dx.$$ For every integer $d\geq0$, substitution $x=4t$ and the beta integral give $$\begin{split}
 \int_0^4 x^d\,d\nu(x)
 &=\frac{4^{d+1}}{2\pi}
     \int_0^1 t^{d-1/2}(1-t)^{1/2}\,dt\\
 &=\frac{4^{d+1}}{2\pi}
     \frac{\Gamma(d+\tfrac12)\Gamma(\tfrac32)}{\Gamma(d+2)}
 =\frac{(2d)!}{d!(d+1)!}=C_d.
 \end{split}$$ In particular, $\nu$ is a probability measure. Both $\nu$ and the spectral distribution of $A$ are supported in $[0,4]$. Equality of all their moments implies equality on polynomials and then on all continuous functions by uniform polynomial approximation. They are therefore the same measure. Since <a href="#eq:rigidity-mp-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-mp-law">[eq:rigidity-mp-law]</a> has no atom at zero, $$\widehat\tau(1_{\{0\}}(A))=0,
 \qquad 1_{\{0\}}(A)=0$$ by faithfulness. In the polar decomposition $s=VA^{1/2}$ this gives $V^*V=1$. Traciality and faithfulness then give $$\widehat\tau(1-VV^*)=1-\widehat\tau(V^*V)=0,
 \qquad VV^*=1.$$ Thus $V$ is a unitary.

<a id="eq:rigidity-ordered-expansion"></a>
<a id="eq:rigidity-regularized-powers"></a>
It remains to transfer the identities. For a fixed regularization parameter we will use polynomial approximation in operator norm; we will then remove the regularization using bounded-ball $2$-continuity. For $\varepsilon>0$ put $$x_\varepsilon=s(A+\varepsilon1)^{-1/2}
 =V\left(\frac{A}{A+\varepsilon1}\right)^{1/2}.$$ Fix $k\geq1$. A polynomial $p(t)=\sum_{d=0}^D c_dt^d$ has the literal ordered expansion $$\label{eq:rigidity-ordered-expansion}
 [sp(A)]^k
 =\sum_{d_1,\ldots,d_k=0}^D c_{d_1}\cdots c_{d_k}
       [sA^{d_1}]\cdots[sA^{d_k}].$$ Every summand is a product covered by Lemma <a href="#lem:rigidity-blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rigidity-blocks">13</a>. That lemma and complex linearity yield $$S([sp(A)]^k)=a[sp(A)]^k,
 \qquad S(([sp(A)]^k)^*)=b([sp(A)]^k)^*.$$ For the second identity one takes the adjoint of the expansion <a href="#eq:rigidity-ordered-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-ordered-expansion">[eq:rigidity-ordered-expansion]</a>, conjugating its scalar coefficients and reversing the block order, and applies the separate adjoint identity in Lemma <a href="#lem:rigidity-blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rigidity-blocks">13</a>. Choose polynomials converging uniformly on $[0,4]$ to $(t+\varepsilon)^{-1/2}$. Functional calculus and boundedness of $S$ pass the last two identities to the norm limit, giving $$\label{eq:rigidity-regularized-powers}
 S(x_\varepsilon^k)=ax_\varepsilon^k,
 \qquad S((x_\varepsilon^k)^*)=b(x_\varepsilon^k)^*.$$

The $x_\varepsilon$ are contractions, and their distance to $V$ satisfies $$\norm{x_\varepsilon-V}_2^2
 =\int_0^4
   \left(\sqrt{\frac{x}{x+\varepsilon}}-1\right)^2\,d\nu(x)
 \longrightarrow0$$ by dominated convergence and $\nu(\{0\})=0$. Multiplication satisfies $\norm{xyz}_2\leq\norm{x}\norm{y}_2\norm{z}$ in a tracial algebra. The noncommutative telescoping identity $$x_\varepsilon^k-V^k
 =\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}x_\varepsilon^j
                  (x_\varepsilon-V)V^{k-1-j}$$ therefore shows that $\norm{x_\varepsilon^k-V^k}_2\leq k\norm{x_\varepsilon-V}_2\to0$; the same holds for their adjoints. All these powers are contractions. Using the bounded-ball $2$-norm continuity of $S$ in <a href="#eq:rigidity-regularized-powers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-regularized-powers">[eq:rigidity-regularized-powers]</a>, along for example $\varepsilon=1/n$, proves <a href="#eq:rigidity-power-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-power-identities">[eq:rigidity-power-identities]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### The spectral conclusion

The final step uses boundedness, the identities for powers, and the finite faithful trace. No continuity assumption on the map is needed in Lemma <a href="#lem:rigidity-unitary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rigidity-unitary">15</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-rigidity-unitary"></a>
<div id="lem:rigidity-unitary" class="lemma">

**Lemma 15**. *Let $(\mathcal A,\rho)$ be a finite von Neumann algebra with faithful normal tracial state, let $V\in\mathcal A$ be unitary, and let $S:\mathcal A\to\mathcal A$ be bounded and complex linear. If $a,b\in\mathcal A$ satisfy $$S(V^k)=aV^k,\qquad S(V^{-k})=bV^{-k}\qquad(k\geq1),$$ then $a=b$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rigidity-unitary"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rigidity-unitary</summary>

<a id="eq:rigidity-sine-bound"></a>
*Proof.* We use analytic polynomials whose imaginary parts remain uniformly bounded on the unit circle while their real parts grow near $1$. Rotating these polynomials will bound $a-b$ on each short spectral arc; the trace will then combine the compression estimates without a loss depending on the number of arcs. Write $\mathbb T=\{z\in\mathbb C:|z|=1\}$ and $$H_m(z)=\sum_{k=1}^m\frac{z^k}{k},\qquad
 h_m=\sum_{k=1}^m\frac1k.$$ We first verify the uniform sine-sum bound $$\label{eq:rigidity-sine-bound}
 \sup_{m\geq1}\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb R}
 \left|\sum_{k=1}^m\frac{\sin(k\theta)}k\right|
 \leq B,\qquad B=1+\pi.$$ By periodicity and oddness it suffices to use the angular distance $t\in[0,\pi]$ from $2\pi\mathbb Z$. At $t=0$ the sum is zero. For $t>0$ put $K=\lfloor1/t\rfloor$. The part with $k\leq\min(K,m)$ has absolute sum at most $Kt\leq1$, since $|\sin(kt)|\leq kt$. Geometric summation gives, for every interval of integers $p\leq q$, $$\left|\sum_{k=p}^q\sin(kt)\right|
 \leq\left|\sum_{k=p}^q e^{ikt}\right|
 \leq\frac1{\sin(t/2)}\leq\frac\pi t.$$ If $m\geq K+1$, put $p=K+1$ and $A_j=\sum_{k=p}^j\sin(kt)$. Abel summation gives $$\sum_{k=p}^m\frac{\sin(kt)}k
 =\frac{A_m}{m}
   +\sum_{j=p}^{m-1}A_j\left(\frac1j-\frac1{j+1}\right).$$ Its absolute value is at most $\pi/(tp)\leq\pi$, including when $K=0$. This proves <a href="#eq:rigidity-sine-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-sine-bound">[eq:rigidity-sine-bound]</a>.

<a id="eq:rigidity-real-bound"></a>
For $\lambda\in\mathbb T$, write $$H_m(\lambda V)=X_{m,\lambda}+iY_{m,\lambda},
 \qquad X_{m,\lambda}=X_{m,\lambda}^*,\quad
 Y_{m,\lambda}=Y_{m,\lambda}^*.$$ Continuous functional calculus and <a href="#eq:rigidity-sine-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-sine-bound">[eq:rigidity-sine-bound]</a> give $\norm{Y_{m,\lambda}}\leq B$. The identities for positive and negative powers imply $$\begin{split}
 S\bigl(H_m(\lambda V)-H_m(\lambda V)^*\bigr)
 &=aH_m(\lambda V)-bH_m(\lambda V)^*\\
 &=(a-b)X_{m,\lambda}+i(a+b)Y_{m,\lambda}.
 \end{split}$$ Since the argument of $S$ has norm at most $2B$, we obtain $$\label{eq:rigidity-real-bound}
 \norm{(a-b)X_{m,\lambda}}\leq C,
 \qquad C=B(2\norm{S}+\norm{a}+\norm{b}),$$ uniformly in $m$ and $\lambda$.

<a id="eq:rigidity-arc-bound"></a>
Fix $m$ and any half-open arc $I\subset\mathbb T$ of angular length at most $1/m$, and let $e=1_I(V)$. Choose $\lambda$ to rotate the midpoint of $I$ to $1$. On the rotated arc the argument $\theta$ satisfies $|\theta|\leq1/m$, so $$\operatorname{Re}H_m(e^{i\theta})
 =\sum_{k=1}^m\frac{\cos(k\theta)}k
 \geq\cos(1)h_m.$$ Thus, for $X=X_{m,\lambda}$, Borel functional calculus gives an element $F\in W^*(V)$ equal to $1/X$ on $I$ and to zero off $I$, with $$XF=e,\qquad
 \norm{F}\leq\frac1{\cos(1)h_m}.$$ Writing $c=a-b$ and multiplying <a href="#eq:rigidity-real-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-real-bound">[eq:rigidity-real-bound]</a> on the right by $F$ gives $$\label{eq:rigidity-arc-bound}
 \norm{ce}=\norm{cXF}
 \leq\frac{C}{\cos(1)h_m}=:\delta_m.$$ In the corner $e\mathcal A e$, this says $ec^*ce\leq\delta_m^2 e$.

Partition $\mathbb T$ into finitely many disjoint half-open arcs of angular length at most $1/m$, and denote their spectral projections by $e_1,\ldots,e_{J_m}$. They are orthogonal and sum to $1$. Traciality and <a href="#eq:rigidity-arc-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-arc-bound">[eq:rigidity-arc-bound]</a> imply $$\begin{split}
 \norm{c}_2^2
 &=\sum_{j=1}^{J_m}\rho(c^*ce_j)
  =\sum_{j=1}^{J_m}\rho(e_jc^*ce_j)\\
 &\leq\delta_m^2\sum_{j=1}^{J_m}\rho(e_j)
  =\delta_m^2\longrightarrow0,
 \end{split}$$ because $h_m\geq\log(m+1)\to\infty$. Each arc estimate is weighted by the trace of its own projection; the number of arcs introduces no factor. Faithfulness gives $c=0$, as required. The right multiplication by $F$ and the tracial compression identity apply to arbitrary $a,b$; the argument imposes no commutation condition and uses no particular spectral distribution of $V$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rigidity-unitary-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rigidity-unitary</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:rigidity">11</a>.* Construct $s\in\widehat N$ and $S=R^{\omega_2}$ as in <a href="#eq:rigidity-second-ultrapower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigidity-second-ultrapower">[eq:rigidity-second-ultrapower]</a>. Lemma <a href="#lem:rigidity-blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rigidity-blocks">13</a> propagates the first-letter identities to every ordered block product. Lemma <a href="#lem:rigidity-polar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rigidity-polar">14</a> then provides a unitary $V$ satisfying the positive and negative power identities. Lemma <a href="#lem:rigidity-unitary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rigidity-unitary">15</a> gives equality of the constant images of $a$ and $b$ in $\widehat N$. The constant embedding is trace preserving and faithful, so $a=b$ in $N$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rem-finite-trace-essential"></a>
<div id="rem:finite-trace-essential" class="remark">

*Remark 16*. The finite hypothesis in Lemma <a href="#lem:rigidity-unitary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rigidity-unitary">15</a> is essential. On $H=\ell^2(\mathbb Z)$ let $Ve_n=e_{n+1}$, let $p$ project onto $\mathbb Ce_0$, and let $q$ project onto the closed span of the $e_n$ with $n<0$. The contraction $S(X)=pXq$ on $B(H)$ satisfies $S(V^k)=pV^k$ and $S(V^{-k})=0$ for every $k\ge1$: the only nonzero input coordinate of $pV^k$ is $e_{-k}$, whereas that of $pV^{-k}$ is $e_k$. Thus the power identities hold with $a=p\ne0=b$.

</div>

<a id="sec:cohomology"></a>
### Bounded primitives for Hochschild cocycles

We first apply Theorem <a href="#thm:liouville" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:liouville">10</a> to separately normal cocycles on a type $\mathrm{II}_1$ algebra with separable predual and a faithful normal tracial state. The resulting uniform bound for a primitive will allow us first to remove separability and then to combine central summands. Throughout this section, normality of a linear map means ultraweak continuity, and separate normality has the corresponding meaning for multilinear maps.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Normality and continuity on bounded balls

The continuity needed by Theorem <a href="#thm:liouville" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:liouville">10</a> is the tracial form of Akemann’s continuity theorem (Akemann 1967, Corollary III.10) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[Akemann1967](#ref-Akemann1967)]. We include a direct proof that allows a center and does not require a separable predual. The stronger joint multilinear continuity result for type $\mathrm{II}_1$ factors is (Christensen et al. 2003, Theorem 4.4) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[CPSS2003](#ref-CPSS2003)].

<a id="stmt-lem-normal-two-continuity"></a>
<div id="lem:normal-two-continuity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17**. *Let $M$ be a finite von Neumann algebra with faithful normal tracial state $\tau$, and let $B:M\to M$ be bounded and normal. For every $R<\infty$ and $\varepsilon>0$ there is $\delta>0$ such that $$\norm{x}\le R,\quad \norm{x}_2<\delta
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \norm{B(x)}_2<\varepsilon.$$ Consequently $B$ is $2$-norm continuous on every operator norm ball.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-normal-two-continuity"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-normal-two-continuity</summary>

*Proof.* We argue by contradiction. A failure of continuity will give elements with mutually orthogonal two-sided supports whose images stay uniformly away from zero in $2$-norm. Random signs will then contradict the boundedness of $B$.

If the first assertion fails, there are $R>0$, $\eta>0$, and a sequence $(x_j)$ such that $$\norm{x_j}\le R,\qquad \norm{x_j}_2\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad \norm{B(x_j)}_2\ge\eta.$$ Choose $\alpha>0$ with $\norm{B}\alpha<\eta/4$. In the polar decomposition $x_j=v_j|x_j|$, set $$r_j=1_{(\alpha,\infty)}(|x_j|),\qquad
 y_j=v_j|x_j|r_j,\qquad
 \ell_j=v_jr_jv_j^*,\qquad e_j=r_j\vee\ell_j.$$ Here $r_j$ and $\ell_j$ are the right and left support projections of $y_j$. In particular, $$y_j=e_jy_je_j,\quad \norm{y_j}\le R,\quad
 \norm{x_j-y_j}\le\alpha,\quad
 \norm{B(y_j)}_2\ge 3\eta/4.$$ The trace and the spectral truncation give $$\tau(e_j)\le\tau(r_j)+\tau(\ell_j)
 =2\tau(r_j)\le2\alpha^{-2}\norm{x_j}_2^2.$$ Pass to a subsequence for which $\sum_j\tau(e_j)<\infty$, and put $p_j=\bigvee_{h\ge j}e_h$. Then $p_j$ decreases to zero: indeed, $\tau(p_j)\le\sum_{h\ge j}\tau(e_h)\to0$, and $\tau$ is faithful and normal.

For fixed $j$ and $h>j$, the compressions $$(p_j-p_h)y_j(p_j-p_h)$$ converge ultraweakly to $y_j$ as $h\to\infty$. Applying $B$ preserves this convergence. The $2$-norm is ultraweakly lower semicontinuous, as is seen from $$\norm{y}_2=
 \sup\{ |\tau(b^*y)|:b\in M,\ \norm{b}_2\le1\}.$$ Starting with $j_1=1$, we may therefore choose successively $j_{s+1}>j_s$ so that $$t_s=(p_{j_s}-p_{j_{s+1}})y_{j_s}(p_{j_s}-p_{j_{s+1}}),
 \qquad \norm{B(t_s)}_2\ge\eta/2.$$ The $t_s$ have pairwise orthogonal two-sided supports and $\norm{t_s}\le R$. Hence $\norm{\sum_{s=1}^N\epsilon_st_s}\le R$ for every choice of signs $\epsilon_s\in\{-1,1\}$. Taking independent uniform signs, Hilbert space orthogonality of these scalar random variables yields $$\frac{N\eta^2}{4}
 \le\sum_{s=1}^N\norm{B(t_s)}_2^2
 =\mathbb E\norm{B\Bigl(\sum_{s=1}^N\epsilon_st_s\Bigr)}_2^2
 \le\norm{B}^2R^2,$$ which is impossible for large $N$. This proves the asserted modulus. Applying it to differences, which lie in the ball of radius $2R$, gives continuity on the ball of radius $R$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Averaging the last input

<a id="stmt-prop-separable-primitive"></a>
<div id="prop:separable-primitive" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:separable-primitive-bound"></a>
**Proposition 18**. *Let $M$ be of type $\mathrm{II}_1$ with separable predual and faithful normal tracial state $\tau$. If $k\ge2$ and $f\in C_b^k(M,M)$ is a separately normal cocycle, then there is $g\in C_b^{k-1}(M,M)$ such that $$\label{eq:separable-primitive-bound}
 dg=f,\qquad \norm{g}\le\norm{f}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-separable-primitive"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-separable-primitive</summary>

*Proof.* We first construct an averaged cochain that is a right module map in its last input. Evaluation at $1$ will give a $(k-1)$-cochain, and averaging the original equation $df=0$ will identify its differential.

<a id="eq:last-input-average"></a>
Choose the symmetric countably supported measure $\mu$ for which Theorem <a href="#thm:liouville" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:liouville">10</a> applies, and write $\mu=\sum_{s\ge1}p_s\delta_{u_s}$. For a bounded $k$-cochain $\Phi$, define $$\label{eq:last-input-average}
 (P\Phi)(\mathbf a,x)
 =\sum_{s\ge1}p_s\Phi(\mathbf a,xu_s)u_s^*,
 \qquad \mathbf a=(a_1,\ldots,a_{k-1}).$$ The sum converges in operator norm, and $P$ is a contraction for the cochain norm. If $W_j=U_1\cdots U_j$, where the $U_i$ are independent with law $\mu$, and $W_0=1$, induction gives $$(P^j f)(\mathbf a,x)
 =\mathbb E\bigl[f(\mathbf a,xW_j)W_j^*\bigr].$$ Set $$F_n=\frac1n\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}P^j f.$$

We use compactness for pointwise ultraweak convergence. For each fixed tuple $\mathbf x=(x_1,\ldots,x_k)$, all values $F_n(\mathbf x)$ lie in the ultraweakly compact ball of radius $\norm{f}\prod_i\norm{x_i}$. The product of these balls, indexed by all tuples in $M^k$, is compact. Multilinearity is closed for this topology, since addition and scalar multiplication are ultraweakly continuous. Thus some subnet of $(F_n)$ converges at every tuple to a bounded multilinear map $F$ with $\norm{F}\le\norm{f}$.

On any common cochain norm ball, $P$ is continuous for this topology. To see this, suppose $\Phi_\lambda\to\Phi$ pointwise ultraweakly and $\norm{\Phi_\lambda},\norm{\Phi}\le C$. For a fixed tuple $(\mathbf a,x)$ and a normal functional $\rho\in M_*$, truncate <a href="#eq:last-input-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:last-input-average">[eq:last-input-average]</a> after $L$ terms. The finite sum passes to the limit, and the remaining difference has absolute value at most $$2C\norm{\rho}\norm{x}
 \prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\norm{a_i}\sum_{s>L}p_s.$$ Letting $L\to\infty$ proves the claim. Since $$PF_n-F_n=\frac{P^nf-f}{n},\qquad
 \norm{PF_n-F_n}\le\frac{2\norm{f}}n,$$ the subnet limit satisfies $PF=F$.

<a id="eq:averaged-right-module"></a>
Fix $\mathbf a$, and put $B(x)=f(\mathbf a,x)$. This is a bounded normal linear map. For any $R<\infty$ and $\varepsilon>0$, Lemma <a href="#lem:normal-two-continuity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:normal-two-continuity">17</a> provides $\delta>0$ such that $$\norm{x}\le R,\quad \norm{x}_2<\delta
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \norm{B(xv)v^*}_2<\varepsilon
 \quad\hbox{for every }v\in\mathcal U(M).$$ Indeed, right multiplication by a unitary preserves both the operator norm and the $2$-norm. The same bound holds after averaging over any $W_j$ and after taking the Cesàro average. Ultraweak lower semicontinuity of the $2$-norm then gives $\norm{F(\mathbf a,x)}_2\le\varepsilon$ under these input bounds. Using $\varepsilon/2$ in place of $\varepsilon$ proves the required continuity modulus for $T(x)=F(\mathbf a,x)$. The equality $PF=F$ is exactly the harmonicity identity for this map. Consequently Theorem <a href="#thm:liouville" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:liouville">10</a> gives $$\label{eq:averaged-right-module}
 F(\mathbf a,x)=h(\mathbf a)x,
 \qquad h(\mathbf a)=F(\mathbf a,1).$$ In particular $h$ is a bounded $(k-1)$-cochain and $\norm{h}\le\norm{f}$.

It remains to obtain its coboundary identity. Let $\nu_n=n^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\mu^{*j}$ and $h_n(\mathbf a)=F_n(\mathbf a,1)$. Evaluate the original equation $df=0$ at $(a_1,\ldots,a_k,v)$, multiply the output on the right by $v^*$, and average with respect to $v\sim\nu_n$. This gives the exact equality <a id="eq:original-cocycle-average"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 0={}&a_1h_n(a_2,\ldots,a_k)
 +\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}(-1)^j
 h_n(a_1,\ldots,a_ja_{j+1},\ldots,a_k)\notag\\
 &+(-1)^k F_n(a_1,\ldots,a_{k-1},a_k)
 +(-1)^{k+1}f(a_1,\ldots,a_k).
 \label{eq:original-cocycle-average}
\end{aligned}$$ The final term is unchanged because $vv^*=1$. Along the chosen subnet, each displayed evaluation converges ultraweakly; multiplication by a fixed $a_i$ preserves that convergence. Passing to the limit and using <a href="#eq:averaged-right-module" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:averaged-right-module">[eq:averaged-right-module]</a> therefore yields $$0=(dh)(a_1,\ldots,a_k)+(-1)^{k+1}f(a_1,\ldots,a_k).$$ Thus $g=(-1)^kh$ satisfies <a href="#eq:separable-primitive-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separable-primitive-bound">[eq:separable-primitive-bound]</a>. This calculation averages $df$ at the displayed tuples; it requires neither a commutation relation between $P$ and $d$ nor separate normality of the limiting cochain $F$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Removing the separability assumption

Uniform primitive bounds and pointwise ultraweak compactness were used to remove separability in (Christensen et al. 2003, sec. 7) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[CPSS2003](#ref-CPSS2003)]. As in that argument, conditional expectations allow the approximating subalgebras to be chosen independently for each finite set of inputs.

<a id="stmt-prop-tracial-primitive"></a>
<div id="prop:tracial-primitive" class="proposition">

**Proposition 19**. *Let $M$ be any type $\mathrm{II}_1$ von Neumann algebra admitting a faithful normal tracial state. Every separately normal cocycle $f\in C_b^k(M,M)$, $k\ge2$, has a bounded primitive $g$ with $\norm{g}\le\norm{f}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-tracial-primitive"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-tracial-primitive</summary>

*Proof.* Order the finite subsets $E\subset M$ by inclusion. For each $E$, choose a countably generated unital von Neumann subalgebra $N_E$ containing $E$, the finite set $f(E^k)$, and unital matrix systems of every size $m\ge2$. Such systems exist in a type $\mathrm{II}_1$ algebra: the unit can be divided into $m$ equivalent orthogonal projections (Blackadar 2006, Examples III.2.5.4(iii)) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[Blackadar2006](#ref-Blackadar2006)], and partial isometries between them supply the matrix units. Adjoining one system for each $m$ adds only countably many generators.

The restriction of the trace to $N_E$ is faithful and normal. The tracial Hilbert space $L^2(N_E)$ is separable: $*$-polynomials with coefficients in $\mathbb Q+i\mathbb Q$ in a countable set of generators are dense in the generated $C^*$-algebra in norm, and bounded strong approximation makes that algebra $2$-norm dense in $N_E$. The faithful tracial representation thus gives $N_E$ separable predual. Moreover $N_E$ has no type I central part. A nonzero homogeneous type $\mathrm{I}_n$ central summand would inherit a unital copy of $M_m(\mathbb C)$ for every $m$, whereas its $n$-dimensional fibers cannot admit a unital copy when $m>n$. Every finite type I algebra decomposes into its homogeneous type $\mathrm{I}_n$ summands. Since $N_E$ is finite, it is therefore of type $\mathrm{II}_1$.

<a id="eq:finite-set-exactness"></a>
Let $\mathcal E_E:M\to N_E$ be the normal trace-preserving conditional expectation. Its existence and bimodularity are classical; see (Dixmier 1953, Théorème 8) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[Dixmier1953](#ref-Dixmier1953)] and (Umegaki 1954, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[Umegaki1954](#ref-Umegaki1954)]. Define $$f_E=\mathcal E_E\circ f\big|_{N_E^k}.$$ This map is separately normal and has norm at most $\norm{f}$. For inputs in $N_E$, bimodularity of $\mathcal E_E$ gives $df_E=\mathcal E_E(df)=0$. By Proposition <a href="#prop:separable-primitive" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:separable-primitive">18</a>, choose $g_E\in C_b^{k-1}(N_E,N_E)$ such that $dg_E=f_E$ and $\norm{g_E}\le\norm{f}$. Extend it to a cochain on $M$ by $$G_E(x_1,\ldots,x_{k-1})
 =g_E(\mathcal E_Ex_1,\ldots,\mathcal E_Ex_{k-1}).$$ This extension has the same norm bound. If $(a_1,\ldots,a_k)\in E^k$, all its inputs and adjacent products belong to $N_E$. Expanding the differential consequently gives $$\label{eq:finite-set-exactness}
 (dG_E)(a_1,\ldots,a_k)
 =(dg_E)(a_1,\ldots,a_k)
 =\mathcal E_Ef(a_1,\ldots,a_k)
 =f(a_1,\ldots,a_k),$$ where the final equality uses $f(E^k)\subset N_E$.

The common norm bound and the same product compactness used above give a pointwise ultraweakly convergent subnet of $(G_E)$, with limit $g\in C_b^{k-1}(M,M)$ and $\norm{g}\le\norm{f}$. For a fixed $k$-tuple $(a_1,\ldots,a_k)$, the indices of this subnet eventually contain $E_0=\{a_1,\ldots,a_k\}$. Equation <a href="#eq:finite-set-exactness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-set-exactness">[eq:finite-set-exactness]</a> therefore holds eventually along the subnet. Passing to the limit in its finitely many coboundary terms gives $dg=f$ at this tuple. This holds at every tuple, proving the proposition. The choices of $N_E$ and $\mathcal E_E$ can be made independently for different $E$; eventual exactness is supplied by the directed finite subsets. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Central cuts and mixed inputs

Uniform bounds and central decomposition also enter the tensor-product argument of (Pop and Smith 2010, sec. 4) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[PopSmith2010](#ref-PopSmith2010)]. In the present setting we use an explicit homotopy to correct all inputs outside a given central summand before assembling the primitives.

For a central projection $z\in M$, regard $zM$ as an $M$-bimodule under multiplication and put $q=1-z$. In degree zero we use $C_b^0(M,zM)=zM$ and $(d\xi)(a)=a\xi-\xi a$. On cochains with values in $zM$, define the central cut by $$(\operatorname{cut}_z\psi)(a_1,\ldots,a_n)
 =\psi(za_1,\ldots,za_n).$$ In degree zero this means $\operatorname{cut}_z=\operatorname{id}$ on $zM$. Centrality and the fact that $q$ annihilates this module show that $\operatorname{cut}_z$ commutes with $d$. For a cocycle $\psi$ with values in $zM$, a primitive for its restriction to $zM$, pulled back to $M$, accounts only for $\operatorname{cut}_z\psi$. The following homotopy supplies the remaining difference $\psi-\operatorname{cut}_z\psi$ on arbitrary inputs, with a bound independent of $z$.

<a id="stmt-lem-central-homotopy"></a>
<div id="lem:central-homotopy" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:central-homotopy-formula"></a>
<a id="eq:central-homotopy-identity"></a>
**Lemma 20**. *For $n\ge2$ and $\psi\in C_b^n(M,zM)$, set $$\label{eq:central-homotopy-formula}
 \begin{split}
 (J_z\psi)(a_1,\ldots,a_{n-1})
 =\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(-1)^i\psi(&za_1,\ldots,za_{i-1},
 q,qa_i,\\[-2pt]
 &a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{n-1}).
 \end{split}$$ Set $J_z=0$ in degrees zero and one. Then, in every degree, $$\label{eq:central-homotopy-identity}
 dJ_z+J_zd=\operatorname{id}-\operatorname{cut}_z.$$ For $n\ge1$ the degree-$n$ operator satisfies <a id="eq:central-homotopy-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:central-homotopy-bound}
 \norm{J_z\psi}\le(n-1)\norm{\psi}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-central-homotopy"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-central-homotopy</summary>

*Proof.* The norm estimate follows directly from <a href="#eq:central-homotopy-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:central-homotopy-formula">[eq:central-homotopy-formula]</a>. To verify the identity, expand each input as $a_i=za_i+qa_i$. By multilinearity it suffices to use tuples whose entries each belong to $zM$ or to $qM$. On such a tuple with first $qM$ entry at position $r$, formula <a href="#eq:central-homotopy-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:central-homotopy-formula">[eq:central-homotopy-formula]</a> consists of the single term inserting $q$ immediately before that entry, with sign $(-1)^r$; on an all-$zM$ tuple it is zero.

Consider such an $n$-tuple with first $qM$ entry $a_r$. In $(J_zd)\psi$, merging the inserted $q$ with $a_r$ has total sign $(-1)^r(-1)^r=1$ and contributes $\psi(a_1,\ldots,a_n)$. A merger strictly before the insertion has the same arguments as its counterpart in $(dJ_z)\psi$, but the latter inserts at position $r-1$, so their signs are opposite. A merger strictly after the pair $(q,a_r)$ also has the same arguments as its counterpart in $(dJ_z)\psi$, and its merger index is larger by one, again reversing the sign. The merger of $a_{r-1}$ with the inserted $q$, when $r>1$, vanishes because $zMqM=0$; the corresponding merger across $a_{r-1},a_r$ in $(dJ_z)\psi$ vanishes for the same reason.

The left outer terms cancel when $r>1$, with insertion indices $r$ and $r-1$. When $r=1$, both left outer terms vanish because $qM$ annihilates the output module. For the right outer terms, if $r<n$ the same insertion index and the two consecutive differential degrees give opposite signs. If $r=n$, the last input is in $qM$, so both right outer terms vanish. Thus the only surviving term is $\psi(a_1,\ldots,a_n)$, as required because the cut of this tuple is zero. On an all-$zM$ tuple, both sides of <a href="#eq:central-homotopy-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:central-homotopy-identity">[eq:central-homotopy-identity]</a> vanish.

For completeness, in degree one the formula reads $$(J_zd\psi)(a)=-(d\psi)(q,qa)=\psi(qa)
 =\psi(a)-\psi(za),$$ since both outer module actions in $(d\psi)(q,qa)$ vanish. In degree zero both sides are zero by the definitions. This proves the identity in all degrees. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:central-cocycle-correction"></a>
In particular, if $\psi\in C_b^k(M,zM)$ is a cocycle, then $$\label{eq:central-cocycle-correction}
 d(J_z\psi)=\psi-\operatorname{cut}_z\psi,
 \qquad \norm{J_z\psi}\le(k-1)\norm{\psi}.$$ This bound is independent of the algebra and the central projection.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Completion of the proof

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-central-homotopy-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-central-homotopy</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* The zero algebra is immediate, so assume $M\ne0$. Fix $k\ge2$ and an arbitrary bounded cocycle $f_{\mathrm{orig}}\in C_b^k(M,M)$. By the normal reduction in Proposition <a href="#prop:classical" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:classical">2</a>, there are a separately normal cocycle $f$ and a bounded $(k-1)$-cochain $b$ such that $$f_{\mathrm{orig}}=f+db.$$ Let $z_{\mathrm{II}_1}$ be the central projection of the type $\mathrm{II}_1$ summand, and let $z_0=1-z_{\mathrm{II}_1}$. We first partition $z_{\mathrm{II}_1}$ into central projections whose algebras admit faithful normal tracial states.

In the abelian algebra $Z(z_{\mathrm{II}_1}M)$, choose a maximal orthogonal family $(z_i)_{i\in I}$ of nonzero supports of normal states. Its join is $z_{\mathrm{II}_1}$: a nonzero complementary projection would support a further normal state, contradicting maximality. A state is faithful on its support, so each $z_iZ(M)$ has a faithful normal state $\varphi_i$. Composing $\varphi_i$ with the faithful normal center-valued trace of $z_iM$ gives a faithful normal tracial state $\tau_i$ on $z_iM$; see (Blackadar 2006, Theorem III.2.5.7 and Corollary III.2.5.8) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[Blackadar2006](#ref-Blackadar2006)]. The family $I$ may be uncountable. Omit $z_0$ if it is zero, and otherwise keep the entire complementary summand as one piece.

For each piece define the restricted cocycle $$f_i(x_1,\ldots,x_k)=z_i f(x_1,\ldots,x_k),
 \qquad x_j\in z_iM.$$ Multiplication by a central projection is a bimodule map, so $f_i$ is a separately normal cocycle and $\norm{f_i}\le\norm{f}$. For $i\in I$, Proposition <a href="#prop:tracial-primitive" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:tracial-primitive">19</a> supplies a primitive $b_i\in C_b^{k-1}(z_iM,z_iM)$ with $$db_i=f_i,\qquad \norm{b_i}\le\norm{f}.$$ If $z_0\ne0$, the vanishing result for algebras without a type $\mathrm{II}_1$ central summand in Proposition <a href="#prop:classical" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:classical">2</a> supplies a bounded primitive $b_0$ of $f_0$. Its norm is some finite number.

Pull each primitive back to $M$ through the homomorphism $x\mapsto z_ix$: $$\widetilde b_i(x_1,\ldots,x_{k-1})
 =b_i(z_ix_1,\ldots,z_ix_{k-1}).$$ The outer module actions also pull back, since the outputs lie in $z_iM$. Thus $$d\widetilde b_i(x_1,\ldots,x_k)
 =z_i f(z_ix_1,\ldots,z_ix_k)
 =\operatorname{cut}_{z_i}(z_if)(x_1,\ldots,x_k).$$ Define cochains with values in $z_iM$ on all of $M$ by $$H_i=\widetilde b_i+J_{z_i}(z_if).$$ Equation <a href="#eq:central-cocycle-correction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:central-cocycle-correction">[eq:central-cocycle-correction]</a> gives $$dH_i=z_if,
 \qquad
 \norm{H_i}\le k\norm{f}\quad(i\in I),$$ and, when present, $$\norm{H_0}\le\norm{b_0}+(k-1)\norm{f}.$$ There is consequently a single finite constant $C$ bounding the norms of all these cochains; one can take the maximum of the two displayed bounds, omitting the second when $z_0=0$.

For an arbitrary central partition of unity, the map $$M\longrightarrow\prod_i^{\ell^\infty}z_iM,
 \qquad x\longmapsto(z_ix)_i,$$ is an isometric $*$-isomorphism onto the bounded direct product. For surjectivity, the finite partial sums of a bounded family $(x_i)_i$, $x_i\in z_iM$, form a net directed by inclusion of the finite index sets. This net converges strongly, since the central supports are orthogonal; its limit belongs to $M$. For each tuple $(x_1,\ldots,x_{k-1})$, the family $(H_i(x_1,\ldots,x_{k-1}))_i$ belongs to this product and has norm at most $C\prod_j\norm{x_j}$. Let $G(x_1,\ldots,x_{k-1})\in M$ be the corresponding element. Coordinatewise multilinearity shows that $G$ is a bounded multilinear cochain with $\norm{G}\le C$. Central projection commutes with every term of its coboundary, so $$z_i(dG)=dH_i=z_if
 \qquad\hbox{for every central piece }i.$$ Since the projections have join $1$, it follows that $dG=f$. Finally, $$d(G+b)=f+db=f_{\mathrm{orig}}.$$ We have obtained an actual bounded $(k-1)$-cochain whose coboundary is the given cocycle. Hence $H_b^k(M,M)=0$ for every complex von Neumann algebra $M$ and every $k\ge2$, with the actual image of $d$ in the definition of cohomology and with no complete-boundedness hypothesis. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "$↓$finite selection and the first ultrapower $↓$free-group length estimate in $N$ $↓$the second ultrapower and polar approximation $↓$bounded sine sums and the finite faithful trace The Liouville contradiction and its use for cocycles. The first ultrapower uses scalar moment convergence; the norm bound is proved for the exact free family before the second ultrapower. The constants $a, b$ are carried through the trace-preserving constant embeddings, and the power identities hold for every $k ≥ 1$.",
      "figure_id": "fig:proof-map",
      "pdf_page": 15,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:proof-map",
          "exact_quote": "The Liouville contradiction and its use for cocycles.\nThe first ultrapower uses scalar moment convergence; the norm bound\nis proved for the exact free family before the second ultrapower.\nThe constants $a,b$ are carried through the trace-pr",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "other",
      "tikz_pgf_source": null,
      "witness_description": "Flowchart diagram enclosed in a vertical sequence of 5 rectangular boxes connected by downward-pointing arrows with text labels between the boxes:\n- Box 1: \"The fixed walk has asymptotically free moments. A hypothetical nonconstant harmonic-map martingale has distinct support points $a, b \\in M$.\"\n- Downward arrow labeled: \"$\\downarrow$ finite selection and the first ultrapower\"\n- Box 2: \"In $N = M^{\\omega_1}$: exact free Haar unitaries $g_1, g_2, \\dots$ and exact first-letter identities for $R = T^{\\omega_1}$.\"\n- Downward arrow labeled: \"$\\downarrow$ free-group length estimate in $N$\"\n- Box 3: \"$s_r = r^{-1/2} \\sum_{j=1}^r g_j$ satisfies $\\|s_r\\| \\le 2$. In each fixed product of blocks $s_r(s_r^* s_r)^d$, $d \\ge 0$, terms with repeated surviving indices have total norm tending to zero.\"\n- Downward arrow labeled: \"$\\downarrow$ the second ultrapower and polar approximation\"\n- Box 4: \"In $\\widehat{N} = N^{\\omega_2}$: $s = (s_r)_{\\omega_2}$ has a unitary polar part $V$, and $S = R^{\\omega_2}$ satisfies $S(V^k) = a V^k$, $S(V^{-k}) = b V^{-k}$.\"\n- Downward arrow labeled: \"$\\downarrow$ bounded sine sums and the finite faithful trace\"\n- Box 5: \"$a = b$, a contradiction. Hence $T(x) = T(1)x$. Last-input averaging then produces the tracial cocycle primitive.\""
    }
  ],
